Speeches by Mr. and Mrs. Crosfield.

The fist of the Ward tea meetings for the winter season, arranged by the Wornen's Liberal Association, was held in Thewlis-street schools on Tueslay evaning for the members and friends of St. Austin's Ward,and Sankey Bridges. Austin's Ward Secretaries) and Mrs. Osborne (secre- tary to the Association) and a goodly company enjoyed a first-class tea, the catering being the subject of favourable comment all rcutd. Mrs. CEOSIIRLD devoted a brief but interesting address from the chair to a consideration of the Insur- ance Bill. She did not think women folly realized to what an extent Mr. Lloyd-George's scheme was going to benefit them. She was not alluding only to the direct advantages to women who insured themselves, bat to the indirect benefits which they would receive through their husbands. It was all very well to say it was the man who insured, but tee wife would reap the benefit of that provision equally with the himbani in the time of sickness when the bread-winner was com- pelled to give up work fern few weeks. The woman felt the suffering and anxiety entailed by ttose break- downs even more than the man, because she had to obtain nourishment for the invalid and food for the children. She therefore felt the difficulty of the pari- ties more acutely than the husband whe was in bed. The benefits payable under the insurance scheme were not very large, but 10s. per week would generally he sufficient to prevent the home being broken up until the invalid was well again. (Applause). NENN." HEATING SYSTENI.

At a meeting of the House Committee a report was presented by a sub-committee appointed to consider the heating of the whole of the Workhouse buildings. The sub-committee unanimously recommendei the adoption of a decentralized system of heating and damestic bot water supply and the acptance of the estimate of Messrs. Killick and Cochran at .£?,649, including building work. It was considerei that in coosequeoca of the purchase of electric current from the Corporation, there would be an approximate saving of 1,000 tons of slack per annum, and probibly a substantial saving in coal. The water consumption would also be reduced. The nes system of heating woull add greatly to the comfort of the inmate.. The House Committee approved the report and resolved that Messrs. %V. and S. Owen, architects, be instructed to prepare plans and spec lice- tion for the inatalla,ion of the system for submtssicn to the Local Government Board.

On the motion of the CHAIRMAN, seconded by Mr. BENT, the minutes were approved.

THE MASTERS ILLNESS.

Dr. floncarN;U, medical officer Workhouse, reported on November 2nd. that the Master (Mr. J. F. Awbery) was seriously ill. An operation bad been performed, but it would be two or three months at least before he would be able to resume his duties. The House Committee decided that temporary assistance be engaged during the Master's illness.

THREE GENERATIONS HAD CHRONIC ECZEMA. lam-Buk Again Proves to be the Only Cure for this Torturing Disease.

Mrs. Emily Manning, of -2. Ship Lane. Dartford, Kent, is of the third generation in a family afflicted with chronic hereditary eczema. Tier father and grandmother carried the disease to their death-beds. but Mrs. Manning has been saved from that terrible fate by Zam-Buk, which cleared the eczema com- pletely from her system. Telling her remarkable story to a Pressman Mrs. Manning said " Eczema broke oat all over my legs in irregular patches when I was fifteen—over twenty years ago. The disease was hereditary—both my father and his mother were shockingly afflicted to the day of thei,- deatha with the torturing complaint. After covering my legs, the eczema spread to my arms, body, hands and face, and even to the roots of my hair. The itching and inflammation were most severe about my knees, elbows, and finger joints, where the constant movement and friction caused the skin to crack and Fcale. My face, too, was such a shocking sight that I was ashamed to be seen._

" During the first years I had the eczema, I was in service in London. My mistress very kindly had me attended by her family doctor, who prescribed various treatments with no good effect. For eight years I went every week or fortnight to a London Hospital. Amongst others' attended St. John's Skin Hospital. the Euston-road Skin Hospital, the University Hospital. and the Middlesex Hospital. At every place I got the best and most careful treatment, but the result was always the same—no cure—so yen can't wonder, remembering my father's and grandmother's experi- ences, that I lost heart.

" A few years ago I was more terribly afflicted than ever—the itchy, scaly sores were practically all over me—my face being a really awful sight. In my distress I wrote to my sister at Guildford, and she sent me a box of Zam-Buk, telling me to persevere with it, as it would cure me if anything could. I tried the balm first on my face and arms. and got so much relief and benefit that I bad no hesitation in applying the Zam-Buk all over my body. The itchy sores were soothed and my whole skin got much coder. A few weeks with Zam-Buk made a wonderful difference— the scaly sores were peeling away and giving place to new healthy skin and I felt brighter and better than for over twenty years. "My face was first cleared of the dread disease ; then my arms, body and legs became healthy again. until Zam-Buk had removed every particle of eczema and left me with a splendid skin. My experience with Zam Buk has proved that no skin disease is too hopeless for this magic balm." Because of its unique healing power, Zam-Buk should always be kept handy in the home. The balm is so pure and wholesome that it can be used with safety even upon the moat delicate skin whether broken, binised, or sore, b box of Zam-Buk really , for me immediately any spot, rash, or pimple appe.ara ca skin or scalp, saves mother many Lula of anxiety as well as pounds in doctors' bills. All Chemists and Stores sell Zara-Birk at Is. I !d. or 20. 9d. a box.

THE FIRE BRIGADE HANDBOOK. La a newly issued and revised edition of "Th. Fire Brigade Handbook " Mr. James Compton Merry. weather glees many interesting details concerning the history of lire protection in this country. An Act was passed in the reign of Queen Anne (1708 to compel parish authoritirs to maintain fire extinguishing appliances, but little that was effective was done until the Insurance Companies began to work lire engines of their own. The Companies attached name plates to premises insured by them by which the firemen would know if their orin Office was concerned, and these plates may still be seen here and there upon old build- ings. Some of the Insurance Brigades are still kept up, and are to be found, well organized and equipped in such Important cities as Canter- bury, Worcester and Shrewebury. Somewhat tardily. the businers of fire Extinguishing was recognised as a public duty, brigade. were re- organized on. municipal basis, and in the last 25 year. great improvements have been made in fire extinguish- ing, and life saving appliances. Toe steam fire-engine has kern to a great extent superseded by the petrol motor fire-engine, of both of which Messrs. Merry. weather and Stn,, Ltd, of Greenwich, were the pioneers, and during the past ten years this firm has turned out over one hundred and fifty motor engines. In his " Fire Brigade Handbook "Mr. Merrsweather deals exhaustively with the whole science of fire-fight- ing, and the organisatirn, drill and rquipment of volun- teer and private brigades. For those who are, or may he, engaged in tire brigade work, the book affords many practical lessons, and it may be studied with advantage by all who are concerned for the rafety of public build- ings, institution., and private macsicns.

MOTOR FATALITY.

TAXI DRIVERS CONFESSION.

Remarkable Evidence at Adjudged Inquest.

Remarkable evidence was given at the adjourned inquiry held by Mr. S Brighouse at the Warrington Police Station on Wednesday into the circumstances attending the death of Charles Bebbington (12), son of Georgs Bebbington, 313, Winwick-street, who was knocked down and killed by a motor car at Longford Bridge on Wednesday, November let. The irquest was adjourned in order that inquiries might be made as to the driver of the car. Last week- end it came to the knowledge of the police that The car belonged to the Warrington Taxi-cab Comp.syy and at the time of the fatality it was in charge-of Henry Carter. At the adjourned inquiry Carter was represented by ,Mr. Chapman. solicitor, klanchestez The Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls) watched the proceedings on behalf of the Warrington Police and Inspector Lewis for the Lancashire County Police. The Coroner said that after hearing the evidence the jury would have to decide the simple issue—whether Henry Carter was the person who canoed the injuries to the deceased, and it no, whether the injuries were inflicted undersuch circumstances as to justify them in sending him for trial. Evidence given et the opening of the inquest was then repeated. It was to the effect that on November Ist the dn.:eased, with his brother Wilfred, aged 16, and another boy named Martin Horan were walking along Wlnwick-road. When near Longford bridge, Wilfred crossed the road, and his trother and Horan (who was put live a soap box on ashen's) were following him when Wilfrid new a motor car approaching from she direction of Winwick. He shouted to his brother and the other boy, with the object of keeping them back. He ran across and pushed Horan back and then caught bold of his brother's hand to pull him back, when the car struck them and carried Wilfred about six yards and Charles about 20 yards. When Charles was picked up he was found to be dead, having received severe injuries to his head and his leg was broken. Wilfred Bebbington, deceased's brother, stated that be saw the car when it was 25 yards distant. It had fonr lamps on. After the accident the car did not atop or appeer to s'acken its speed. Mr. Chapman raised the question of the lighting of the road and the Coroner raid the nearest lamp would be about GO yards sway. Questioned by Mr. Chapman, the witness Bebbirgton persisted in Ilia statement that the car had four lamps —not two. There was no other traffic about at the titre.

Chapmtto : Why did'nt you take care to put your brother in a place of safety ?—I had cro:sed the road. The Coroner : This boy new the car coming at top speed 25 yards away. What could be do? Mr. Chapman : lam going to suggest that be would hear the car long t afore he could see it. The Coroner : In face of the information you have in your possession of what your client admits in his statement to the police, what is the use of thrashing 000 a small matter of detail?

Mr. Chapman : I don't know what he has admitted. The Coroner : First of all, he has admitted that he did it. Secondly he has admitted that he took the car to his own house and left it at the back of the house for the night instead of taking it to the garage. Thirdly be admits be got the ear repaired in the morn- ing so as to deceive his employer. Fonrthly, he deiiveiel the car to his employer without saying a word about the accident, and fifthly he falsified a statement to his employer as to where he was and actually booked the time up to three o'clock in the morning. He deceived everybody. He denied what was the actual fact—that he seas there. lie was interrogated by the police. He kept up the deception by lies until he got Into a corner and had to admit it.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

'The Girl in the Train."

Warringtou people hare flocked in large Lumbers this week to see the latest musical success "The girl in the Train," which is adapted from the German of Victor Leon, and possesses some continental features which never fail to attract. Perhaps it is a reflection on us as a people that we should show so much appreciation of musical plays from the continent which are essentially " spicey " ; but there is evidently something in even the staid British composition which enjoys being moderately shocked. "The girl in the I Train" was doubtless an exceedingly proper young person, though an actress ssith the stsgey name of Gonda Van der Loci, but her midnight journey in a railway sleeping compartment with a married gentleman as sole compar.ion not unnaturally caused trouble. The proprieties had to be discussed and satisfied in the divorce court and the unfortunate but well-meaning gentleman, who had flown to the aid of beauty in distress (and got locked in the " sleeper ") was divorced on his outraged wife's petition. All this sounds very eerious. but in the play it is treated very lightly—too lightly for Mrs. Grundy, who is notoriously a biased person but in a way which keeps the audience in a very hilarious mood from beginning to end. There are only two scenes, the first being in the Divorce Court, where the defendant and the intervener (Miss Gonda Vander Loo), and the sleeping-car attendant all give evidence before a President who persistently burlesques the proceedings and shows how farcical divorce might be if handled in the Gilbertian style. Act 2 takes us to the house of Mr. Van Buren, the divorced husband, where the actress is a guest at a private hall, and is followed by the susceptible Judge. Even the wife turns up again, and eventually becomes reconciled through the medium of a love duet with her husband " which has to be sung and acted to throw dust into the eyes of papa, just returned from a vryage. With her various adroirers,Gonda has a particularly lively time, and Miss Stella Gastelle makes the most of the role. As the wife, Miss Dorothy Arliss acts with considerable grace and charm, and in both scenes is seen to decided advantage. President Van Eyck, the philandering Judge of the divorce court, lets down the law hut keeps up the humour of the proceedings in merry fashion, and one has nothing but praise for the indefatigable efforts of Mr. Bay Russell, a versatile comedian whom the part snits well. Mr. Harry Brayne is ale) very funny as Cornelius Scrop, the droll conductor of the sleeping car, and indulges in some amazing passages with Martje (Miss Minnie Hall) the coquettish maid-servant to the Van Burma's. As we have indicated there is no end of fun and frolic. but the music is decidedly sketchy, and beyond such duets and songs as "Oh, married life," and "Oh sleeping car," there is not much to charm the ear. A con- siderable amount of dancing is sandwiched between the musical numbers and the burlesque, and in this 'direction the plincipals all distinguish themselves. Musical people will be disappointed with "The Girl in the Train," but those who seek nothing more than a merry evening will get their fill of laughter.

MISS GORNALL'S CONCERT.

At Mi3s Mary Gornall's matinee concert in Bold. street schoolroom on Thursday a preg,atztue of classical iostrumental and vocal music was performed which attracted a fairly numerous audience, of which the great majority were ladies. Miss Marjorie Wigley and Miss Amethe Leadbetter—pianist and violinist respectively—wets the instrumental soloists, and the playing of each of these ladies evidenced nrueicianly qualities of a high order. The Beethoven sonata No. S. in which both were ass dated, is of more than ordinary interest in itself, and these two artiste's played the four movements if which it is C Dlil prised sympathetically and with a cleat perception of their varying and contrasted moods. Miss Leadbetter produces a tone which, though perhaps not of the fulle4 and roundest, is delicately graded and tenderly expressive, while her splendid technique enables her to invest with a peculiar charm whatever she essays, her beautiful legato playing in Schubert's "Ave Maria," with its fascinating passages in double-stcpping,stand- ing out as one of the most striking examples of her artistic interpretations. In Miss Wigley's playing of Liszt's Rhapsody Na. 12 one discerns qualities which entitle the possessor of them to high raak as a piano exponent. Gifted with the true artistic temperament, Miss Wigley showed too, a breadth of p mer,allied to degree of restraint, which enabled her to deal in a way equally delightful and convincing with both Liszt* robustness of thought and Chopio's graceful idealism. Mk. Hilda D'Angelis sang same groups of German and English songs in which were found two -" Das Kraut Vergessenheit " (Eugen 1 1 il,(aell) and "A. Birthday" (('owen),—wbieh commanded attention by their intrinsic merit and by tha depth and subtlety of expression which Miss D'Angelis threw into their interpretation, despite the fact that her soprano voice is somewhat hard and unsympathetic in quality. Of Miss Hilda eragg-James one can unhesitatingly say that her earnestness of style and her rich contr alto voice make a powerful appeal to those who hear her, her rendering of the excerpt from " Sams3a and Delilah" (Saint faun.) and of " Die Ma;nacht (Brahma) being such as to leave a most favourable and lasting impression inregard to her vocal resources Miss Goreall and Mrs Llewellyn Davies shared the duties as well as the honours of accompanists, for b Ali these ladies showed the best of taste in support ing the vocalists and the solo violinist.

HOW AND WHERE TO STORE DAHL! k ROOTS. Where to store the tubers is a matter that may chi- cern the novice, but this may be disposed of very quickly by saying] that they may be stored where they are free from frost and damp. The place must not be overwarm, or the roots may be encouraged into developing growth, whlcla is fatal to their well-being. the roots are frequently placed under the staging of glass structure welch Is jolt sufficiently heated to exclude frost. They are more often stored in a dark cellar or spare room or dry , shed, but always where the root• base ample protection against frost. It must be dry without • tendency tr. get damp at any time. When placed under the staging of • greenhouse, therein elways the risk a drip reach- ing them, causing them to densp off or else enceorreging premature growth when the tubers should le quite dormant. Lech grower should adapt hims...f to hie circonsstences, devising means whereby full rotectiou can be provided.—.` TM Gordon:

ROME AND ITS FORUM.

MR C E. PARKER'S IN

" Rome sod its Forum " was the subject of a highly interesting lecture given by Mr. C. E. Parker before the members of the Warrington Society at the Old Academy on Monday evening. Mr. Parker's recent visit to the " Eternal City " enabled him to present a vivid impression of the Forum and its surroundings as they appear to-day and he also dwelt upon the rich histoi ical associations of a spot which has been famous from the very dawn of civilization. Mr. F. R. ROBERTS, who presided, said Mr. Parker had selected a noble theme and they had every confidence in his ability to handle it. Mr. PARKER began his lecture with some general remarks upon Rome and its influence in the world. My earliest knowledge of Rome, he said, came t me not from a study of Rome, its history, architectwe, law, or language; but from a book, or rather a litera- ture of one of the p oples who occupied an outlying proviuce of this great old world Empire. I need , scarcely say that I refer to the sacred books of the Jews, commonly cal'ed the Bible. emptied itself into the river Tiber at the Palatine Bridge. As originally constructed, it was no doubt metely a natural stream cleaned out and side walls built. In later times a circular sewer admirably built called the Cloaca Maxima was made. It is is use at this day. It is 14 feet broad inside measurement and 20 feet overall. The inner tiog was constructed of stone and tw3 outer rings of brick. This sewer was no doubt a convenient receptacle for rubbish of all sorts, including bodies of those killed in the Forum. The Forum is two and a half acres in extent. It is traversed by the winding street called the Via Sacra which commences under the Capitol hill, the low end of the Forum, and winds its way by a somewhat devious course, rising at the south end, where it loses itself somewhere about the Arch of Titus. . As we see it at the present day the Forum is largely a heap of ruis. True, many interesting fragments, mostly foundations remain, but it will be necessary for us to try, with the aid of history and the researches of archamlogy, to re-construct in part at any rats, hem these foundations and broken columns the Forum as it existed about the beginning of the Christian era. You will remember that Cesar Augustus, or as be originally was called Octavius, was the relative and heir of Julius Ctesar. He came into power along with Mark Antony at the time of the breaking up of tha Roman Republic, and the establishment of the Empire. For twelve years he shared the rule with Mark Antony, and for forty-six years he ruled alone as Emperor, dying a natural death (a rare thing for a Roman Emperor) at the advanced age of seventy-six. A. man of versatile genius ; one I f the greatest although not the best Emperor Rome ever had, it was his proud boast that he had found Rome brick and left it marble, and he bad good reasen to make this boast. There is however little of the marble now left in the Forum, or in the Coloseum either for that matter, but if we could only identify it we should find that much of the marble used in the churches of Rome was originally in the buildings of the Forum, or the Coloseum. Only a portion of the buildings in the Forum are due directly to Augustus. Traversing the Forum, Mr. Parker then gave interesting descriptions of the various temples and objects of interest.

The god Juturna was the nymph of water, and then hre presided over the springs of the Forum. A temple existed in the Forum to this god over one of the springs near the temple of Vesta, and its waters were cared for by the Vestal Virgins. Mr. Parker tou-hed upon many other outstanding features of the Forum and concluded by expressing the hope that what he had said would stimulate some to careful and de'ailed study of a little tip ton the earth's surface which was so rich in the historical associations of a great peop'e. MODERN CIVILISATION-A PESSIMISTIC OUTLOOK.

Councillor JENKINS proposed a vote of thanks to the lecturer who, he said, had taken their minds back to the ancient Roman Empire with all its greatness and its methods of education. The Romans showed tremendous perseverance in fighting and conquest and in their endeavour to develop what they believed was the highest form of national government in the world. What did we see in our country to-day ? If we walked for a few moments away from the main thoroughfares we saw filth and misery among a vast number of our population. What result was education and the refinement of the few producing ? Day by day he met hundreds of children in penury and want, who evidently came from wretched and miserable surroundings. And we arrogated to ourselves that we English stood in the forefront of the endeavour to ameliorate the human race. Was it all humbug ? Were we &mined, like the Roman Empire, to sink into oblivion until future generations were reminded of our former greatness by lectures like they had heard bat night? Let them hope it might not be so. Mr. A Mt/ENFIELD, in seconding, emphasised the point which Mr. Parker had made with reference to family life being the foundation of the Roman Empire and of the conception if what a State ought to be. After all we were a large family and if we c old only bring into our affairs the principle of brotherhood - the thought that we had a central hearthstone and were all related to one another - it would help us to solve many of the problems of the present day. THE TRIPOLI OUTRAGES.

The CIIAIRMAN said that if one were asked to pick out the equare mile of the earth's surface which had teemed most with life and loomed largest in the story of the world, he thought the Forum of Rome would be the selected place. One could not help contrasting the glory of ancient Rome with the miserable exhibi- tion of cowardice and cruelty of which the descendants of that race had been guilty in Tripoli within the last few days. It made one wonder whether, with all our boasted civilization, we had advanced very far. There was a great deal about our modern civilisation that would not bear inwstigation, and we might learn much from the history of the ancients. The v..te was heartily accorded.

THE LAND DI WHICH WE LIVE. At this time, the-time of Rome's glory, the inhabitants of these Islands,together with the whole of the Northern part of Europs, lived in huts in the forests and wastes of the conntry. In contrast to these huts the Romans were erecting buildings, the architecture and work- manship of which have ever since been the wocder of the world. Rome was rightly called the Mistress of the World, and the decree of Caesar Augustus that "all the world should be taxed," was no empty boast but the expression of an authority which had to be obeyed Nor hod this power expressed itself merely in force and in conquest. Taking it as a whole the lordship of Rome over the then known world was beneficent. When Rome was at her zenith the despotism of Eastern monarchies kept a large portion of their people prostrate on the ground. The narrow policy of the Greeks limited the blessings of freedom in their own land. ROME DISTRIBUTED lIIE,E BLESSINGS TO lIIE WORLD. She considered the conquered countries as so many nurseries of citizens, included them in her legions,end admitted the nobles into her senate. Several of her most able rulers were not Roman born. Along with her laws and franchise she communicated to them her arts, sciences, and language. Wherever the Roman eagle penetrated, schools were opened; aqueducts, bridges, and temples built; and all the powers ond knowledge of architecture, sculpture, and painting, were used to adorn the principal towns in the con- quered provinces. Roman law, which althounb severe, was just, was established throughout the Empire, and when Rama like a Colossus ceased to stride the world consenting not lone adopted her laws as their general code. Amongst the material expressions of her greatness the

0011A0 ROADS take an important place. These roads, the many remains of which exist to this day radiated from Rome or more exactly from the Golden Milestone in the Forum, to the extremities of the vast Empire, and all the differcnt nations that composed it were linked together, not only by the name laws and government, but also by the facilities of intercourse and communi- cation. And even in her decadence when Rome was stripped of her imperial honours and power, she again renewed her benovelent efforts and sent out. not armies to conquer, but apostles and teachers to christianize. By them she RoRE THE LIGHT OF CHRISTIANITY AND LEARNING into the dark recesses of idolatry and ignorance ; made conquests more enduring than those gained by the power of the sword. Such was R .me at her beet. Proceeding to concentrate attention upon THE FORUM Mr Parker said : Cicero, the great Roman orator, in speaking of Athens said "In this City that is a thing w inexhaustible,which for whichever way we move we tread mein some history or other." This observation is more applicable to Rome to-day and especially to the Roman Forum, than it was to Athens in the days of Cicero. The Roman Forum is one of the spots where historic interest largely centres. Students of Roman history find it mentioned in almost every chapter. Every yard of it has again and again been drenched with human blood. Here Cicero spoke, Ca-oar ruled and Horace meditated. These Forums were originally market places. They naturally became the centre of the public life of the cities, and public buildings corres- ponding to our Town Halls, Police Courts, Churches, Ac.; were built around the Forum. Gradually there- fore the markets were ousted, as all the room was required for other public purposes. There are several other Forums in Rome to which the markets were itiadaally transferred, and the Roman Forum became the site of buildings of an administrative and judicial nature, and of open spaces of a public character. Hence the development of Rome as the Capital of the world came to be mirrored in the Forum. The study of this Forum is complicated by the succession of public edifices which at different periods occupied the same ground. Remember that for over twelve hundred years this Forum was the centre of the life of the oity and Empire. The origin of the Forum AS a public meeting place dates back to the alliance between the Romans and the Sabines. We are

THE ROSTRA.

On the right of the Arch of Severna rises the massive blocks of the Rostra, or orator's tribune. Here Mark Antony hung up the head of Cicero after his murder, and the wife of his old foe Clodius spat in the dead face. The Rostra was a long raieed platform on which the speaker could walk to and fro. Oratory was thought much of in Rome and all sons of patricians were tau* public speaking. The Rostra wasa kind of Town Cress of the c.iy. At the death of any prominent citizen the body was carried before the I Rostra on a bier and his funeral oration given from the platform. Here Mark Antony delivered the famous discourse over the body of Julius Caesar. Opposite the Rostra was the open space flagged with marble in which the crowds would gather to hear the speakers and debates. Mr. Parker briefly touched upon the efforts of archrcologists from before the Christian era onwards to locate the tomb of Romulus, the founder of Rome, and said there was no definite proof on the point. THE SENATE HOL,3E.

On the northern corner of the Forum was the Curia

_ or Senate House, corresponding to our Houses of Parliament. The Central Hall had seating accommo- dation for six hundred persons. This was the Senate Hall in which the fate of the countries in the world was often decided. It was here where Cicero often addressed the foliate. It was destroyed by fire caught from the funeral pyre of Claudius. The site, or at least part of it, is now occupied by the church of St. Adrian°. The level of the floor of this church Is 20 feet above the floor level of the Senate floor. When Christianity was established in Rome, burial of the dead instead of burning became the practice, and the wall face of the Curia was converted into a catacomb, and the open space in front, called the Comitium, into a cemetery. The remains of more than four hundred bodies were found in this part during txcavations,aad some stone coffins still make up part cf the wall which now forms the foundation of the church wall.

TEMPLE OF VESTA.

Vesta was the pekes of light and purity, and all the victims offered to Vesta were *bite. You can still see the seats of honour in the Colosseum reserved for these Vestal Virgins beside the Empress. They bad the privilege of sparing the life of any victim in the arena, and indicated their wish hv turning their thumbs upwards. The Temple of Vesta was four times burnt and four times rebuilt, the last time in the 15th century. The temple was circular, as were most early temples before the Grecian, with its rectangular forms of buildings, dominated Roman architecture. The idea of the family as a unit gathering round its own hearth became the basis of ' Roman law. The family was the unit of the State, and the associations of these family units became the family of the state. The Stets religion and State law, organized by Name, the successor of Romulus, regarded the body politic as one househeld, made up of the family units cl which the father was the repre- sentative. The hearth in the Temple ,f Vesta in the Roman Forum became the visible family nucleus, and the fire that burnt there Isymholized the unity of the State. When a new colony or village was founded embers were carried from the central fire to the new hearth, and by this the connection of the of the colony to the central body was preserved. Mr. BRATA&ONT Of the Manchester Anti-Suftrage Society, who conducted a correspondence with Miss Waring on the suffrage question in our oolumns, said his objections to the enfranchblement of women were aurnmned up in three main points. (I) Law and order was only secured by tte minority being subject to the rule of the majority. (2) The essential recognition of the mysterious, hut nevertheless Inalterable and wide diversity betwaen the sexes. (3) Toe undesirability of conceding the importance of far reaching legislation in response to militant demands. He said the question had often cropped op about the right to vote. He contended there was no such thing as the right to vote. If there was finch a thing as the right a man would he able to dispose of it as property. Resuming the debate Miss STERLING referring to the argument of physical force said that the whole birth of the world had tended to a profound conviction to the contrary. 'Muir very lives were firml7 based on the conviction that human beings bad a spiritual and moral nature mere powerful than the physical. She did not believe ther 3 Rea the slightest fear of the men turning out and breaking the law because they believed it to be woman, made law, and she failed to see the slightest evidence of it being likely. Miss MOIR in reply, said she agreed with Miss Sterling that the world was not governed by physical force. She never for one moment said it was. It was I the element of pliysical force which men at the bottom ,of good government. If they were to be governed by spiritual force what secs the necessity for the vote It was pretty generally known that women had higher ideals than men. If that were so it would be a danger to government to give the sex Who had a higher ideal the power of making laws and to force those laws on , the sex who had lower ideals. The grave danger was that having those high ideals ahead of them they would get laws which a man wetild not keep and therefore bring the whole of the laws into disreapect.Before they should have the vote women should learn more about the political question and she would suggest they began with municipal government. The tendency had always been for the two sexes to differentiate whilst the tend- ency of the suffrage movement was to become assimilated. They could not demand protection and equal rights and responsibilies. (Applause.) rho debate was wound up by Miss Sterling who said if the suffrage was granted to women it 'maid tend to consolidate what had gone on very markedly for fifty years—the sharing of life in its fulled sense. The resolution was pot to the meeting by the CHAIR- MAN, a majority icing declared in favour. A vete of thanks io tte Chairman brought a pleasant and educative meeting to a close.

EVERY MAN TO VOTE.

PREMIER S MOMENTOUS ANNOUNCEMENT.

Woman Suffrage Coming.

PLURAL VOTINC ABOLISHED

Franchise Anomalies to be Swept Away.

An announcement which will mark an epoch in the developmeot of free institutions in ibis country was made by Mr. Asquith on Tuesday in receiving a deputation on adult suffrage. The Government, said the Premier, wmld next semi vi introduce a Bill conferring the vote on every man in the community and abolishing plural voting. The measure thus foreshadowed sill be the most notable extension of the franchise since the great Reform Bill of 1832. At the 1901 census there were in England and Wales alone 8,552,436 males and 0,547,629 females of the ages of 21 and upwards. The age divisions of the numbers enumerated at this year's census have not yet been made known, but the population of England and Wales showed a total increase in the last ten years of 3,547,426. The nuinber of electors at present on the register in England and Wales is 6,387,716. Manhood suffrage would add about 3,000,000 to the number of voters in England and \Vales. The number of women of the age of 21 and upwards in England IA present must be 10,000,000 at least. On the question of woman suffrage Mr. Asquith re-affirmed his old position that it sill be open to the Hcuse of Commons to make that extension by amendment. . . . .

It is probable that the qualification for the vote will be sir months' residence. The position when the deputation met the Vrime Minister was : .

1. That plural voting would be dealt with next session. .

2. That reg'stration was being considered, and 3. That a week, or possibly longer, would be granted for the Conciliation Bill. By one stroke Mr. Asquith will demolish all these perplexities. The entire network of existing qualifications will disappear (says the " Daily Fevre.") With those qualifications will vanish the sinkter figure of the plural voter. Men will vote once only, not by virtue of a latchkey, but as citizens. The Bill will be no fiamed as to allow an amendment extending the vote to women. Such amendment will be left to the judgment of the House. If, however, it be carried, the Bill, including women's suffrage, will remain a Government measure, to be supported as such on third reading. It will be quite a short Bill, and need not occupy any more time than would be necessary to deal s,parately with Omit voting and women's suffrage. The Bill will place the duty of registration ~n a public authority, and will so sweep away the costly battles now annually fought out before revising barrister 8.

THE HISTORY OF FOOTBALL

When was football first played in this country ? The " Philadelphia Record, "in an interesting article on football, seta out to answer thin question At Derby, England, football was played an the memorial of a great rejoicing for a signal victory gained over the Roman legions in the third century. At Chester the first football used in an annual gams is said to have been a Dane's head. In the Isle of Purheck the free quarrymen have from time immemorial per- petuated their claim to a piece of land by 4:ek- ing a football /ICT ON it in accordance w.th the quaint old tenure of the original grant. The football bobbed up serenely during the reign of Edward 111. of England, when, in 1319, the game was prohibited by public edict because it diverted time and attention from the more martial and patriotic exercises of those feudal times, such as archery and wrestling. This, as far as it is kowein, was the first mention of the game by the popular name it bears now. Edward 111, however, had previously forbidden struggles over " grossess pelotes " (big balls) and Henry IV. and VIII. also legislated against football as a had thing for the " dear peemil. " It seems that even Shakespeare attacked the game, although the closest student of that poet may find it difficult to find the passage in "King Lear" in which reference is made to a "bane football player."

===== 0002601_19111111_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
A PATRON SAINT.

- - Footballers may also be interested to bear that the game has a patron saint: Although it is not generally known, the football bag a patron saint. In 1520 a boy named fingh, who wee one of the champions of his day, had the misfortune to kick a ball through the window of a Jew's house. Whether the ball struck the man or any member of his family in not known, but certain it is he van very much incensed about the affair. Determined on revenge he enticed the unsuspecting Hugh into his home and plur ged a knife in the youth's back. Ike English people were very much cut up about the loss of their champion. albeit not as much cut up as Hugh. They malady punished his slayer, made the young football player a saint, and gave him a big nod expensive funeral. They ' even wrote series; bad Terns, but still verses, describ- ing the virtnes of "sweet idir Hugh " and his prowess as a football player. Football was forbidden in Elizabeth's reign under pain of imprisonment, the reason Icing the extreme brutality of the game, we are told. And James I. debarred " all rough and violent exercise. like foot- ball " from his Court. In spite, however, of the pro- hibition against the game, the London apprentices often kicked a football about the streets to keep them:prima warm in winter, and it is on record that la the mavens frost of 1661' the London streets were "fall of foot- balls."

HELP (?). (Whilst professing to support the Insurance Bill the Opposition are doing all they can to prevent its speedy passage into law.)

THE INSURANCE BILL: "I say, Balfour, are yjm supposed to be helping him to carry me?"

From Reynolds' Newspaper.

tion of British Treasury experts—when you come presently to consider the adjustMent of Ireland's finances in the forthcoming Home Rule Bill. MR. BALFOUR'S SHAKY HISTORY. Mr. Balfour condescended the other day to offer some remarks on the now inevitable Home Rule Bill and since he gave a good deal of time to the historic side of the ques- tion it may be interesting to follow him in that domain. We were glad to find him acknowledging that aspect of the question;for your average Unionist bluffs the case by talking contemptuously of the musty for gotten past. But where in the world did the Leader of the Opposition—the Leader by pleasure of the Halsbury Club—get his patchwork theory of history from? He blandly tells us that the Irish Parliament failed. It is common ground between all the responsible writers of whatever political colour or leaning that the peace, commerce and general welfare of Ireland were higher than ever in the period of that Parliament.l The Parliament was ended, Mr. Balfour says,' and with the bland manner of an old-fashioned lecturer before children calmly ignores the infamous bribery and corruption by which that ending was compassed. Of course every- thing depends on the point of view and if the object of granting a Parliament to Ireland in 1872 was to keep that country backward and derelict, then indeed it was a failure. For Mr. Balfour cannot but know that in their secret communica- tions the devisers of the Union avowed that one of their principal motives in endeavouring to abolish the Irish Parliament was the stoppage of the prosperity of the country. Cooke, the Under-Secretary, in a most secret memorandum for Castlereagh's use states some of the causes of the dis- advantages of the existence of an Irish Parliament. He enumerates six causes, and as the second " disadvantage " we have this statement :—" The general prosperity of the country, which has produced great activity and great energy." This document was revealed to the public in 1849, and a more vivid example of the base conspiracy which sought to check the "energy and activity" of the Irish race cannot be imagined We regret there is no space to follow further Mr. 13alfikir's strange interpretation—if so partisan and inaccurate a rendering may be thus termed—but may we point out to Englishmen his surprising ignorance of the British Constitution itself. Speaking of the Canadian and other Parliaments in the Empire, he said these were absolutely independent and free from control of the Imperial Parliament in Westminster. We make no pretence to a legal mind, but we should have thought any student of Dicey or Anson—the accepted authorities of the Con- stitution—would have known that no Bill which the English Ministry opposed can become law in any Colonial Parliament, and Mr. Balfour must have known in his own lifetime of the use of this Veto. To cite only a few, in 1872 the assent of the Crown was refused to a Canadian Copyright Act, because parts of it conflicted with British legislation. A year later a Canadian Act was vetoed as being contrary to the British North America Act, and on similar grounds in 1878 a Canadian Shipping Act was vetoed by the Home Government. Similarly in our own day, Parliament has disallowed Australian Acts for checking Chinese immigration. We think it extraordinary that an ex-Prime Minister should show such want of accuracy in so important a matter, not of Ireland, but of the British Constitution itself.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST,

AN FXTRAORDINARY LAPSE.

On Thursday evening, the Rev. George Curtis wa, publicly recognised as pastor of Hill Cliffs Baptist Church. Last night, in connection with St. Clement's Illation, the Rev. Prebendary Wakeford lectured on " Marriage." The two events remind me of a singular incident. An aged couple, personally known to myself, being in receipt of old age pensions, arranged to marry and so effect a saving in rent and so forth. The prospective bride undertook to put up the banns and make the necessary appointment as to officiating clergyman. Calling at the parsonage one evening the Lund that the vicar was away, therefore ebe decided to leave a message with the good man's wife. The latter happened at the moment to be con- ducting a class of young women, and the visitor was invited into the room where there were gathered. When she simply explained the nature of her errand, the audience naturally became all avention, though of course there was an abundance of decorewdy suppressed merriment. " Well," went on the vicar's wife, " and what is the happy man's name ?" " As, deary me," responded the puzzled thane "now you bother me. for I've clean forgetten it ! " " But furtly," said the questioner, " you can recall it ? Come, just think now, and don't be flurried—take a little time." " Nay, it's gone; for sure it has. Well, this here is a bonny come off. Hold on a minute. , It's sumroat to do wi' windows, I know that much." Thhs the ancient dame; whereopm there ensued quite an exci,ing guessing competition. At length one girl suggested curtains 'As, bless yo, child," exclaimed the visitor, " that's done it—for sure it has. His name's Curtis !" " WHOOA EMMA ! . . .

Thursday was Mayor's day, not only at Warrington, but at every other place boasting a Charter of Incor- poration; and great and marvellous are the stories that such an occasion brings to mind. There is the classic instance of the "self-made "functionary who ol served that a Mayor must be "like Clesar's wife, all things to all men," and there are innumerable other chestnuts of the kind. There is one perhaps not al well known to the effect that once upon a time the late Queen Victoria accompanied by Princess Beatrice, visited a provincial town for II e purpose of opening a certain new public building. Immediately after the ceremony Ber Majesty was invited to place her signature in a book provided for the occasion. After she had done so l'rincess Beatrice followed suit, and then the pen was handed to the Mayoress of the town, and she was asked to sign too. Dipping her pen into the ink she was for a moment non-plumed by observing that t he signatures preceding hers were simply

" VICTORIA." " BEATRICE."

However she was equal to the oecriFion, and, getting a good grip on her pen, she triumphantly arrawled on the next line— _

" EMMA JAN."

Our own borough is remarkably fortunate in having, not only a Mayor who adorns the office, but likewise a Mayoress who, in all circumstances, lends grace dignity and charm to the jenition. MISQUOTATIONS.

The cne cited in the openi g senterces of the fore- going item .-eminds rue of the paragraph on "Biblical quotations" in last week's " Women's Chat." S. meone has noted that a pleasant hour may be spent now and again with a good dictionary el quotations. It is indeed delightful to turn over the pages haphazard and refresh one's memory with these jewels of thought gathered up from the treasure of English literature. The pity of it is that so few will take the trouble to look up their quotations ; men are too ready to accept them Loco each other instead of going to the true s urea. As the compiler of the Women's column showed a week ago, Bible quotations are frequently in error; yet what a simple matter it would be to verify them for oneself. As other writers upon this fruitful theme have observed, we often hear that the tongue, according to the Scriptures, is an " unruly member," but you may search the Scriptures from end to end without finding the passage that describes it. I have a good collection of such curiosities, but cannot make much one of them in these miscellaneous jottings. Some time ago, in a article dealing with favourite misquotations, it was pointed out that the octil lure pas-age coming nearest to the popular phrase about the " unruly member " is—" But the tongue can no man tame; it is an unruly evil." One of the most general of Biblical misquotations (con- tinued this authority) is or was, " Charity covereth a multitude of sins,"for the authorised reading,"Charity shall cover the multitude of sins " ; however the revised version has " Love covereth a multitude of sins" ; so, after all, there was some authority for the emendation. The Prayer-book offers no such excuse fi r variants ; yet dna men out of ten talk in all good faith of a" just cause or impediment" when " cause or just impediment "is what ia written ; and among the laity it is usual to substitute "for ever after hold his peace " for " hereafter for ever." In reference to the marriage service many make the mistake, " Those a horn Cod bath joined," instead of "joined together." And so forth and so on ; it would be possible to multiply such things- almost indefinitely. ELLEN TERRY. I have touched on two or three current local topics, but I take it that of its kind the leading event of the week iu Miss Terry's Shakesperian recital at the Parr Hall on Thursday evening, so that I need hardly apologise for adding to what I have previously written respecting this ever-green subject. Mist Ellen Terry's autobiography, reprinted from an American maga- zine, has been published by Messrs. Hutchinson. It is full of anecdotes and incidents in her long and distinguished theatrical career. As I stated last week, she began her life upon the stage with Charles Kean and his wife when she was only eight years old, and afterwards joined a stack company. At sixteen years of age she was married to G. F. Watts, the at tist, and with him spent a brief holiday at Tennyson's house iti the Isle of Wight. Of this visit she writes at length and very interestingly. She also deals with dignity and admirable taste with the story of her brief married life, concerning which many inaccurate stories have been told. As I have previou,ly noted, her gifts as an actress are inherited, and, moreover are, in some degree shared by her three sisters, two brothers, and children. As I have already shown, she was only eight years old when she made her first appearance on the stage under the Keane management, taking the part el the child, Mamilius in " A Winter's Tale " at the Princess Theatre. Soon afterwards she played as I'uck et the same theatre. Two years later she was taking a part in "King John," and performed it in a fashion that won hearty recognition. In the early sixties she was with various of the old " stock " companies in which she gained a great deal of valuable experience. In 1887 she acted for the first time with the late Sir Henry Irving, playing Katherine to his Petruchio in "The Taming of the Shrew," at the Queen's Theatre. In 1875 (between 1888 and 1874 she was absent from the stage) she played Portia in an eleborate revival by the Bancrofts of the "Merchant of Venice." It was tier brilliant performance that rescued the revival from failure. A succession of triumphs followed. The lustre of these led to her engagement by Henry Irving as his leading lady for the Lyceum in 1878. Thee begun that long partnership which extended from that time until 1902, and won both great renown not only in England bat throughout the United States (which she visited for the first time in 1883), and on the Continent. It was a partnership that marked a splendid revival of Shakespeare, "the chief glory of the actor's calling." Miss Terry showed her versatility and genius in wonder- fully varied rolee—Ophelia in 1878, Portia in 1879, Beedernona in IhBl, Juliet and Beatrice in 1882, Viola in 1884 Lady Macbeth, Katherine (Henry VIII.), Cordelia, Imogen, Volumnia Ac. &c. Those who heard her here on Thursday will assuredly concede that the gifted settees reaches her zenith in Shakesperean characters—embodies the poet's women as he drew them, plays them as he meant them to be

played. Testifies one critic—" As Portia and Beatrice her special gifts of method and temperament found their chief 'trope, making the Lyceum a name of world wide not,. Miss Terry played in many other parts, but it is as a most charming and accomplished Shakes- perian actress that she will always be remembered. Finally: Her sense of fun is keen. She was once asked by a lady hairdresser for a testimonial for some hair-wash. Miss Terry consented, and the hair- dresser's surprise may be imagined when she received a large portrait of the actreis as Marguerite in " Faust." Of course in this part she weals a wig with beautiful long plaits. Beneath the photo was written—" Ellen Terry, after rue application of Miss 's hair-wash." She has her foibles sod prefer- ences. For instance, she collects the eye-glasses and pinee-nez of celebrities. She dislikes Ibsen plays. She loves perfumes and rich fabrics. She does not care much for jewels. She is very fond of cats, and trains them to perform a number of clever tricks. Warrington is proud to have been honoured by her presence this week, and the Philomathic Society has hereby added to its laurels. TEMPERANCE SUNDAY. Temperance in relation to intoxicants will be specially considered in local pu'pits to-morrow and the day's units d efforts; will culminate in a representa- tive meeting in Parr Hall, arratged by the executive of the Band of Hope Union. The ref mice sends me back upon the annual repo] t of the Commissioners of Customs and Exci,e, to which allusion has already been made in this column. According to this , unquestionable authority, the consumption ofopirits which had been on the cleciine for the last ten years, fell at an accelerated rate daring the twelve months under revi-w, no that now it is but half what it wan at the beginning cf the decade in question. Neither does the Englisl man drink nearly as much beer as he did. Curiously enough, there has likewise been no inconsiderable falling off in coffee; and, while there has been increased demand for mineral waters and cocoa, tea has made by far the greatest advance. But when due allowance is made for the larger consump- tion of the various temperance beverages, it is plain that the nation does not drink anything like so much as was the case a few years ago. Nevertheless, there is still plenty of scope for reformers, and the most sanguine and optimistic of these will agree that the time for the abolition of Temperance Sunday in not yet. It is true that within 83 recent a perk das the year 1899 the people spent on intoxicating liquors , the appalling sum of £186,000,000 or so, and that the total now stands at £157,000,000. This is indeed, as Carlyle used to phrase it, "significant of much," especially is it a matter for congratulation and encoeragement when, glancing over statistics concern- ing mortality, one finds that deaths from cirrhosis of the liver and other diseases unmistakably due ti exceesive drinking are markedly en the decline. All the same, goodness knows that for vast strides in the way of further improvement there is ample room aid verge enough. I will nit trouble readers with the comparative tables, but I will just remark in passing that, when in this respect set side by side with her ! daughters acmes the seas—say, Australia, New Zealand, and in particular Canada—the L'aited Kirgdom has muse lks.r shame and confusion of face. THE CLUB QUESTION.

must not pursue this imrortant subj. et to the exeusion of other interests, but 1 should like just to touch uptn the aspect of it indicated in my sub- headline. From a Blue Book issued in August of last Tear, I find that on the let January, 1909, there were in England and Wales 91,045 premises with on and off licences, and 24,677 premises licensed for the sale of intoxicating 'ignore for consumption off the premises, giving proportions, according to the ekimated population, of 26.3 en-licenct s, and 09 off- licences per 10,000 persons. The number of on-licences showed a decrease of 1,472 compared with , January Ist of the previous year, the number having: steadily fallen from 100,766 in 1903, and it may fairly be assumed that this has meanwhile continued at an equal rate at least. I tupgest this bemuse in ten years 1895 to 1904 the average annual decrease was 380. For the four years 1905 8 the average was 1,358, and for the five years since the Act of 1904 came into operation abut 1,381. The decrease of off-licences will probably be about 245. Now I come to my main poit,t. It wag stated that at the beginning of the year above mentioned there were 7,323 registered clubs, and it was estimated that these were increasing at the nits of 312 per annum. Commenting upon this, one wrote—" If that estimate is realised the increase will be the greatest in any recorded year, and will be a matter of much impot t- ance. The figures do not support the common state- ment that for every public-house that is closed a club arises, that being still a more than four-fold exaggera- tiou, but they bting it appreciably nearer the truth, 'and show that clubs are increasing at a greater rate than can well be accounted for by the growth of population." However, the estimate was not realised, and the pessimisti3 Dote thus struck was not justified. Take, for instance, the last annual report of the U.K.A. Ina sec:ion dealing with the increase in the number of clubs in its relation to the increase of on-licences, it is therein shown that there has been no such increase of clubs as is commonly stated to be the ease, and that the great majority of the new clubs do not enter into competition with the public-house. The importance of the club as a liquor distracting agency is stated to be greatly exaggerated to withdraw !popular attention from the public-house.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Tligir Majesties' Visit to India.—As may be imagined, everything in the way of sumptuous appoint- ments has been does to make their Majesties voyage to the East in the Medina,pleasant and asreeable. A novel feature is the provision of specially fitted apartments amidships, for the use of the Queen in rough weather, which is rather to be expected in the Bay of Biscay at this time of year. The Medina, which sails on Slaw- day, escorted by a powerful cruiser squadron, is the latest and largest of the P. &0. liners. She may well be termed a floating pa'ace. and the vessel has been fitted with a specially powerful wireless installation, no that at no psrt of the voyage will she be out of touch with the rest of the world. Her full complement with mariners and band, numbers close upon 800 persons. Their Majesties suite and guests make ups party of about thirty, and the ladies in attendance on ihe Queen include the Duchess of Devonshire, the mistress of the Robes ; Lady Shaftesbury, a Lady of the Bed chamber ; and Mims Venetia Baring a Maid of Honour. .

The Revel Indian Tour—On their previous Indian tour the King and Queen visited Delhi, when they were the Prince and Princess of Wales, but their Majesties' Durbar visit to India is an event of historic import- ance, and will be the occasion of universal rejoicing throughout that vast Empire. The Durbar will be the last of a series of Coronation ceremonies, as it will ha, from the spectacular point of view, the moat magnifi- cent, and it will be the first time in history that the reigning Sovereign and his Consort have held Court in the capital cities of India. The great Durbar will be held on December 12th, but their Majesties will stay some eight or nine days in Dalhi, where Lord and Lady Harding° will entertain an immense party in the Vice- regal Camp. Among a very large nember of distin- guished penple going out to India era the Duke and Ditchers of Hamilton, Lord Iveagh, Lord and Lady Gerard, L3rd and Lady Londeaborough, Lord and Lady Mar and Kellie, and about of other titled folks. The King and Queen will ho in Calcutta about Christmas time, and the capital of India will witness one of the most brilliant Anglo-Indian winter seasons on record. Christmas Fare.—So many commodities premise to be exceptionally dear at Christmas time, that it is 53me relief to be told that turkeys, p3ultry, and game of all kinds, will be more than usually pientifol, and ought to be cheap. The fine, dry summer prevented the loss of young birds from disease, end these favourable con- ditions also prevailed is Normandy and other countries, from which we import turkeys in large quantities, and we are informed that they ought to te from 3d. to 4d. per lb. cheaper than last Christmas. The Christmas pudding on the other hoed, will cost a good deal more. for all the articles that go to the making of that indis- pensable item of Christmas fare--dried fruits, sugar, butter, eggs, etc., are up in price. Nuts also are scarce, and likely to be dear. At present walnuts which the " Lancet" tells us are a " ridiculous excess" when taken after dinner, are p'entifol, but the dry year has tended to produce kernelleas nuts, and the crops gener- ally are reported to be small. swapping by Telephone.—People with up-to-date notions are fad of eating that they could not exist without the telephone, but it has many-disadvantafes, One of them is that your friendly telephoneless neigh• bourn are apt to come in at all hours of the day and night, to make use of it, and you are liable to be called up by some enterprising shopkeeper announcing that he is holding a bargain or removal sale, and that it affords you the chance of a lifetime. Another new form of advertising is the practice of shopkeepers weighing in "conversation lozenges "with your goods, which when you pick them out, invite you to " try our home killed bacon "or some other article, as the case may be. That of course has nothing to do will the household telephone to which the gravest objection in the temptation it affords the housewife to do her shopping over the 'phone. The shopkeepers approve of it,but as everybody knowe, it is apt to produce very different results from those when the housewife goes herself to the fishmonger, the butcher, and the greengrocer, and Bees beforehand, the goods she is buying. . .

Palmistry.—There are few businesses more profitable than that of the palmist fortune teller with a fashion- able clientele, cad there is a fresh scare among seers of all varieties, concerning the attitude of the Public Prosecutor, towards their profession. The palmist or clairvoyant is usually a woman affecting a mysterious name, and a semi-Oriental style of dress, and there ia generally an air of sumptuousness abont the rooms in which she carries on her profession. Otherwise there is little to distinguish her ant from that of the fortune- telling gypsiea, whom the law classes with rogues and vagabonds. The practice of palmistry goes back to very ancient times, but it is perhapa a little surprising that such a discredited profession should flourish in these enlightened days of universal education and higher intelligence. _ .

Natural Waists and Corsets.—During the past two or three weeks, many conferences have been held on the subject of fashions, but nothing of importance has resulted. Simplicity is the leading note of many of the models now exhibited from the best Paris houses. Draperies show a master hand in their arrangements, and individual touches are introduced in trimmings that red a further charm to gowns that would be considered lovely without this addition. An understanding with regard to the fashionable figure has been arrived at during the conference mentioned, and fashion for once has made a rational and sensible decision. The waist for this winter is emphatically natural,and accords with the healthy lives women now live. Ten years ago, a small waist was the height of almost every woman's ambition. But now, if Ibis is the result of tight-lacing, it is rightly considered, a distortion. A complete revolution has taken place in consequence of this change of opinion, in the corsets women wear, which no longer crush the ribs and injure the lungs, but fulfil their appointed mission in protecting the sensitive parts of the body. A carefully chosen corset—which need not be an expenaive one—makes all the difference to the apecarance and the wear of a dress. So many good designs are to be had to-day that the smallest areas allowance will permit of a good cut and fit, provided a little discretion is used in the choice.

Novel Combinations.—Dresess of quite fragile materials in bright colours will be worn under long dark for or velvet coats, or if the skirts are of heavier materials, bodices of folded chiffon, coarse lace or biznrre embroideries find favour. Lnce appears this winter on serge suits as well as on gowns. It is worked in thick threads for the adornment of the former. Very crude are some of the decorations used in simpler form in cross-stitch ca the newest dress bodices. Vivid green, brilliant orange, purples and reds run not on most inconguous grounds, but when colours are subdued In tone and they contrast or combine with the colour of the dress, this ornamentation is not without merit.

Mancheetet has now a storage of water equal to 49 days, the supply having been increased by four days an the result of Monday's rainfall.

IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

MISCONCEPTIONS ABOUT IRELAND. That Home Rule is" good business" for England and the Empire, as well as for Ireland itself, is a truth which has perhaps not been sufficiently emphasised in the propaganda throughout the country. The number of men of affairs who are ignorant of the most elementary facts of the economic history of Ireland and of her financial rela- tions with Great Britain, is legion. Such ignorance is not surprising and in our opinion the fact of such ignorance is not without argumentative force for a change in the present system of ill-informed Govern- ment. The misconception so common in the English commercial mind that "Ireland is a pauper and a beggar dependent to-day as she has always been through the centuries on the bounty of Great Britain and that her poverty is due to the incapacity of her people land their inaptitude for commercial pursuits" is no doubt very distressing to anyone at all informed on Irish affairs but that the mis- conception does largely prevail is evident in the pages of the " Spectator " and in conver- sations with even Liberal business people. The story of the destruction of Irish indus- try and commerce, though known to every tyro who has glanced at Irish history, is perfectly unfamiliar to those, and they, we fear, are the majority of Englishmen, to whom that history is a sealed book. Such a history—and Mr. Balfour's incursion this week on the historic side of our question shews clearly that it is a most important factor—such a history we say might well have a greater gloom of commercial deficiency and want of industrial habit in its useful train. HOW TRADE WAS STRANGLED.

But the moral is doubled and trebled in force when the blight of the era of penal restrictions and the decadence of the greater part of the nineteenth century are set over against the picture of the progress achieved by Ireland during the eighteen years of her own Parliament's legislation.- in-the reign of William 111. the "Deliverer "of "glorious pious and immortal memory" the great woollen industry of Ireland was ruthlessly destroyed. When the Irish people found that the Pension Parliament of Charles 11. which like its king, was in the pay of France, had suppressed their cattle trade, they commenced to make preserved meat and bacon and as soon as that trade showed prGsperity the industry was attacked by English statesmen and similarly destroyed. Cotton, glass, iron, hats, sugar refining— every industry to which Ireland turned was destroyed by England by the imposition of prohibitive duties or by the closing of ports. One by one " as the late Lord Dufferin truly said "each of our nascent industries was either strangled at its birth or handed over gagged and bound, to the jealous rival interests of England." And even Mr Balfour has delivered this striking criticism,not very long ago, "There was a time, a very unhappy time" said Mr. Balfour " when the British Parliament thought they were well employed in crushing out Irish industries in the interests of the British producer. Itwas a cruel, and it has proved to be a stupid policy." Against this undisputed part of Irish history, let us now set down the turn events took when the Irish were given charge of their own purely Irish concerns. From the very moment Grattan's parliament was established there at once came on foot a great Irish industrial revival. Historians agree that the years of Grattan's Parliamert were marked by a commercial and industrial advance quite unparallelled at that time in any part of Europe, and men in Ireland who had no sympathy with the native element in that country, testified to the extraordinary advance in material prosperity. Even the Bankers of Dublin were moved to record on their minutes in 1798 this resolution, which passed unanimously, that " since 1782 the commerce and prosperity of this kingdom has enormously increased." Clearly then all the destructive measures on Irish industries by a mistaken English legislature had signally failed to strangle the capacity of the Irish people for commerce and industrial advance.

The further fiction which presents the picture of an Ireland " beggarly " and "dependent" is with an amusing kind of logic being much circulated by those whose first principle it is that Irish Government by Westminster has been an unqualified success. Apart however, from this illogical bungling, let us look at the statement itself. Does the London Jingo who grows ecstatic with delight over the offer of two Colonial Dread- noughts know that on returns from the British Treasury, Ireland, after paying all dues and demands for her own government, has during the last century paid into the British Exchequer no less a sum than three hundred and thirty millions? Three Hundred and Thirty Millions! Think of that fact— we say fact advisedly, since it is no wild statement of an "agitator," but the computa-

The Examiner Aturday November 11, 1911

NOTES BY " OVALIST."

The match of the season is expected at Wilderspool to-day, the crack Wigan team being the visitors. Will Warrington win is the question? Personally I think they a ill. The players are determined to put in every ounce, and what that means at Wilderspool visiting teams well know. So far, the Wirepullers have not lost a point on their own ground this season, and they mean to maintain the record. A year ago to the day they beat Wigan by 12 points to 10, after a great struggle in which they were unquestionably the superior side.

Looking up the report of the match I find that the Warringtm team was short of Fish and Shugars, that Brooks was injured in the first quarter of an hour and played as a sort of forward while Thomas worked the pack, and that Wigan got a lucky five points in the last five minutes. The try of the day was scored by Waywell. May he repeat the feat.

Virarrington'a team that day was inferior, in my opinion, to the side selected to do duty to-day. The thirteen pla3 era a year ago were : Tilley; Bradshaw, Dickenson, Taylor and Waywell ; Lloyd and Brooks ; Thomas, Boardman, Morgan, Poison, Chesters and Naylor. Wigan can hardly be said to have improved, and the odds are certainly on the side of the Wire- Fullers.

The eelected Warrington team is: Tilley, full back , Waywell, Dickenson, Bradshaw aud Woodburn, threequarters ; Brooks and Creevey, halfbacks ; Johnston, Thomas, Shugars, Arnold, Cheaters, and Bradshaw for cards.

This is about the best side Warrington can command though I should have preferred Cusworth to Dickenson for attacking purposes. However both men played well last week, and the difference between them is slight. Dickenson's chief fault is in lying too far back, behind the scrummage. We want the three- quarter line close up with every roan on his toes. There should be a rare duel at half-back, where Brooks and Creevey ought to hold their own with the famous Wigan pair. As Todd will be an absentee there should be little to choose between the two threequarter lines.

Important changes have been made in the team which into represent Wigan. In last Saturday's game naainst St. Helens, both Ramstlale and Todd were injured. but it is expected that the first-named will have sufficiently recovered to figure in the ranks against the " Wirepullers." Price, however, ban been selected to take Todd's place in the three- quarter line, whilst Mark Green has been substituted for Myers in the forwards.

The selected team isr—Sharrock Leyth m, Jenkins, Price, Miller; Weave, Thomas; Seelinc, Ramsdale, Williams, Sikock, Winstanley, and M. Green. Reserve, Myers.

Prior to the match there there will be a school- boys' game between St. James' and Silver-street ; kick-dl at two o'clock.

Last Saturday, Warrington gratified their supp,rters with the biggest win away from home which they had recorded for sune time. Seventeen points to seven was the t ,tal at Swinton, and but for the referee's strictness on what he considered forward passing, the Warrington score would have been nearer 30 points. In every department the team played fine football, and the passing and running was the best seen this ssason Woodburn got tfflo fine tries ia the first quarter of an hour, and proved that he only wants posse at the right tine to be a free-scoring wing. Brooks played a great game, and Creevey was consistently clever, though he obviously avoided taking rialcs. Among the forwards, who all did well, the stars were Arnold and Cheaters. The former is becoming a second O'Neill in regard to his cleverness in getting the ball out of the ruck and among the threeqnartsrs. He paved the way for two of the tries on Saturday.

The weakness of the opposition had a lot to do with Warrington's easy win, of course Swinton bad some of their best men away, and for a club with such poor reserves this was serious. Oily Parker and Griffiths displayed any cleverness behind, though the forwards were energetic. Kick and rush was the policy of the Blues, and this did not pay against the speedier and cleverer men from Warrington. The famous Lions have lost all their ferocity, and the young cubs will have to grow considerably before they restore the prestige and power of the old club.

Since Saturday Rochdale Inee defeated Widnes by 8 points to 3. and Oldham won at Runcorn on Wednesday by 14 points to 5.

The "A" team to visit Wigan will be: Jordan lull back ; Clare, Unsworth, Vernon and Porey, threequarters ; Lloyd and Daintith, half backs ; Harmer, Cooper, Robinson, Critchley, Gibbs and Newton, forwards. I don't fancy their chance, but the championship is hardly within reach anyhow !

The Frawley case is becoming farcical. Hull are willing to pay 5.,1.50 for his transfer from Warrington. but the Colonial managers say they are going ta hold Frawley to his agreement with them. It looks very much as if the famous threequarter will have to spend the winter in idleness. No one seems inclined to give way.

The Australaaians areemphaaising their superiority over Northern Union footballers in a fashion which admits of no doubt. They have wen every County match and International, beaten the Northern League, and on Wednesday they wen the first test match at Newcastle, the chosen of the whole Union going down by 19 points to 10. They were considerably out- clued in play, and I think it ought to be fairly admitted by now that the Colonials play better football—more scientific and effective football—than any of our N.U. sides. They have improved on our own game till they can beat any team we put in the field. The defeat at Wigan. I consider, a slip and would still " back the Colonials to beat the Wipners three times out of four.

The LEr--rurtge. said h. thought that there was a certai I hope that the people of Warriegton would take the matter of the Town Planning up. Within two years of the poising of the Act no much interest Lai been arousal in it by the solid kind of men they wanted, namely, those on the committees of Town Councils, thtt one hundred of their local authorities in Great. Britain were seriously considering town planning. (Apploise.) Hs wished to mike it quite clear that town plauciug was not a dangerous policy, but a wise fend common sense method of dealing with town develop- ment. They must first ask the permission of the Local Government Board to prepare a plan. He hoped that in another ten years they would not have to seek for permissio-. to prepare a plan but that they would be told by Paeliament that they had to prepare one. The area should include what they could rightly call greater Warrington, including those parts, irrespective as to whether they were in the boundary or not, but where many of the business men of the town lived, and by inviting the co-operation of ths Rural Councils Warrington could prepare the plan and include. the area. Until they had obtained the permismon of the Local Government Board, the only thing they could do thoroughly was to get the Council and owners in agreement about the general desirability of preparieg it. They must call • conference of owners and place before them what they proposed to do in regard to the Act, the amenities of the area, sanitary cormitions and things of that kind, then apply to the Loco! Govere.- meat llotrd ani start on the second stage—the actual preparation of the plan. No roads must be made except. in accordance wirh that plan, and the lines must be the lines embodied in it. Many had the ides that it was • wiste of money to prepare this years before it was required. The Act simpty said that when an estate developed it should do no along certain the Cr and definite lines. There were three typei of roads that should be developed—the main arterial road, the secondary road anl the residential road. The first named should lead out of the town and shoull be of suole a width that would meet a real modern !seed. The " road hog " had come to stsy and it was essential that wider roods should be provided to : mNeet the needs of soco vehicles. He did not mean, : however, that they should have a road of 200 feet wide, ; such an idea was hopelessly absurd. If they wanted a road of such an abnormal width, they must be prepared to pay for it. Toe secondary road would bee road on a par with those of today, being &boot 54. m. wide, :with shops &C. Such • road would act sa a service road for the residential road. And then the residential road —where he hoped that the houses would be net back, and where a littls peace and quietness could be obtained. This read be hoped would be • cul ds sao land a-, minimise the traffic. What a boon this would he to some people ! A WILLI working all the day among machinery did not want to Lear the sound of noisy traffic constantly passing before his home. What was wanted instead of the gray roads was the green ones. The former cost a great deal of motley to maintain but what could be better than the green which did not cost much and which wag more pleasant to maintain. Then there was the limitation of the number of houses to the acre. If there was one thing to which • man should stand true it was in regard to the giving of had for recreation purposes. Tr.ere were 40 houses to the sere to-day but he thought that 20 was quite reasonable. The ides, however, that towns were going to extend miles and miles away was foolish for they oust look after the industrial towns and the interests of those ecgsged in these industries.
