Following upon the Colonials' triumph over Swinton on the Wednesday, there was any amount of confidence among the %Farrington men that they would repeat the dose on Saturday. Swinton have been unfortunate of late, and the team has suffered from injuries to lead- ing players. On Saturday Del Davies, D. B. Daviee, Fairhurst and klorria were all semntees, and the reserves sere hardly of the calibre to oppose Warring- ton, who had only one change from the selected team— Unsworth for Bradshaw. She weather was, fortunately fine, though the ground was soft as the result of heavy rain the day before. A crowd of about 3,000 witueseed the match. Teams end officials :

Warrington Tilley, back :;* *Woodburn, linsworth, Dickenson and Way well, threequarter tacks ; Brookes and Creevey, half-tacks; Johnston, Thomas, Shugars, Arnold, Chesters and Bradshaw, forwards. Swinton won the toss, and had the advantage of a alight breeze, but Warrington almost at once set up an attack. Thomas, Johnston, and Araold led the way to the home line in dashing fashion, but a penalty against Brooks lost the advantage. S sinton could make little headway, and when Creevey broke through at a fast pace a score for Warrington seemed certain. Luckily for the bomesters, Griffiths charged the ball down near tie line, but the defence did not hold out long. Brooks got the ball away smartly, and pretty pissing by Creevey and Unsworth gave Viroodb3rn a chance which he took with both hands and raced over at the corner. Thomas failed at goal. On resuming, th 3 locals made. better show, but there wee no combination behind the pack. Parker made a Mut run, and threw to Valentine but the big centre was smothered before he could get up speed. Loose forward play harressed the W3r- rington defence and when Waywell hid a kick charged near the line, ha was very lucky to save. Tilley finally cleared with a huge punt, and Johnston following up well took the ball past GI iffiths to the home quarter. Here Warrington's combination told a tale again. Arnold threw out from the ruck and a repetition of the handling by the Lame backs saw Woocburn go o'er again a' a flue pace. Ih3 angle was again aide, and Thomas failed at goal. Swinton rallied and were grantod a penalty In a good position, and some of the deficit was wiped off when Griffiths success- fully negoti Ited the goal kick. This apparently was an unexpected reverse, for Warrington again got dangerous, mainly through sterling forward work and the understanding between Brookes and Creevey who got the left sing going, and Dickinson served Waywell at half way. The wing man twice threw forward in gathering, but be never let the ball down, and after a floe ran he scored at the corner, amid fruitless appeals from some of the home players. No. goal. A moment later, Waywell again raced clear after fine hack play, and on reaching Griffiths punted over the line Griffiths downed his man, however, and the referee ignored the incident, which certainly robbel Warrington of a fair try. Half- time : Warrington, 3 tries (9 points) ; Swinton, 1 goal (2 points). Swinton restarted the second half and made a b.tter show than at any time during the initial stage. Valentine and Jones made ineffectual attempts to cross, but the Warrington defence was very sound and Tilley reared his lines. Swinton were not allowed to remain at the Warrington end loaf., for Arnold, fielding the ball splendidly, raced along the touch and Swinton's defence was sorely taxed. There was a tendency to infuse too much vigour and on one occasion McVeigh was caulht in the act of kicking and promptly sent off the field. Play rattled at centre for a spell, until Arnold got the ball and roan ling. the home forwards gave Woodburn a fine pass on the night. The wing man raced away, and near the line threw to Cheaters who doehed over with a capital try, Woodburn failing at goal. Swinton now rushed the game to some purpose, Gartrell and Valentine forcing their way to the Warrington quarter where Parker took a poss and scored a grand try after a 30 yards run. Griffiths landed a goal, and lor a moment the locals pot up a fight. Warrington soon showed superiority however, and Creevey raced round the posta, only to be recalled for a forward pass from &agars. Then Brooks made aroaple of dashing runs,Waywell walking over the line, bathe referee was against the point once more. There was no holding the Warrington men, how- ever, and the halves again threading their way through the home backs, Dtcicenson took a pass close In and scored with ewe. WORKS BOWLING ASSOCIATION.

DISTRIBUTION OF PRIZES.

A Record Year.

The Warrington Works Bowling Association brought a record season to a close on Saturday evening when the prizes gained in the annual handicap were distributed at a smoking concert held at the head- quarters, the Horse and Jockey Hotel. There was a large attendance of members and the gathering was characterised by that spirit of good fellowship which is always to the fore in the associat,on. Captain H.R. Croßfield (president), presided and was supported at the cross-table by the Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph), the Deputy Mayor (Alderman Forshaw), Mr. Harold Smith, P., Messrs. E. J. Hall,Peter Bate,R. Higham, J. Willett (chairman of the committee), W. Patrick (secretary) and J. A. Rushworth (treasurer). Tbe MAYOR proposed the health of " The President," who, he said, saes always at the forefront of every! movement which condoned to the god of tha corn- 1 enmity et large. Whether it was a smoking concert or some other gathering, he was always ready to help to the utmost of his genial manner' II befitted a member of so honourable a family as tbeCrosffelds. (Applause.) The Warrington Works Bowling Association, be con- tinued, was an instance of the good feeling that existed between the various industrial concerns in the town and their employes. During his term of office he had bad a 'striking illustration of the good effects of that feeling in connection with the railway strike. As long as that spirit prevailed, they need not fear that there would be any wrest disturbance in Warrington, even if they had strikes. They would always try to settle their differ- ences amicably ithout resorting to anything in the way of violence. (Applause). The toast was sccorel musical honours._

Captain CROSPIELD, in responding, endorsed every word the Mayor had said aa to the value of a game like bowl,: which brought them all together. When they met on the bowling green or at convivial gatherings they forgot all about such nasty things as soap, wire and leather—(laughter)—or eves about the Corparation —(laughter)—not that they did not have very merry times in the Town Council occasionally. (Laughter.) He wished to congratulate the Chairman (Mr. Willett) and their very bard-working secretary (Mr. Patrick) on the successful year they had just concluded. It was a reCo'd year. (Applause.) Fifty learns entered from 39 works-11 more than lA4 year—and in the handicap their were 483 entries-123 mnrg than last year. That was a tezord of waich they could well be proud. One feature about bowls was that people did not go off their heads when playing it. (Laughter.) When the devotee of golf got off his game, he went about looking as if he did not love anyone. (Laughter.) Then again some over-keen motorists—the motor Banda and road hogs—did not seem to get any pleasure unless they went out to kill somebody. (Laughter.) After playing bowls, everybody went away happy. Ile was always talking about Germany, and he was pleased to point out that we in England hada very solid advantage over the Germans in that we played bowls and they bad to be content with nine-pins. (Laughter.) Possibly In the future some of those present might like to go over to Germany and teach them how to play howls. What did they think of a committee of the Wilmington Works Bowling Association playing before the Kaiser? (Laughter.) Touching upon more serious questions with regard to Germany, CiptaUl Crosdeld said be had recently twee told by a British military attache( who spent considerable time in Germany that the Germans thought we English were moribund. In the near future, added his informant, we would either base to climb down or wake up. He (the speaker) hoped we would wake up. (Applause). In oonclusion Captain Crosfield said the Mayor's presence was only another instance of the way in which he was throwing himself int) the life of the town.

The NIA vort,thenidistribnted the prizes as follows : The winners of the first four were: 1, TlJackson (Messrs J Crosfield and Sons), cup, clock and bronzes; W Langley (Messrs Rylands Bros. Ltd.) ; 3, E Blackledge (Ryland. Bros.); 4, ft Stvory (Rylands Bros.). Over 100 orders on tradesmen sere distributed. Mr. HAROLD SMITH, M.P., in proposing the toast of "The patrons and vice-presidents," described Warring- ton as one of the most sporting towns in the most sporting county of England. (Applause), lie had always held that the working classiest cf Eng- land got all too little recreation and amuse- ment and it was therefore a magnificent thing that the united works of Warrington should form such an association as that. It conduced not only to the success of the great game to which they were devoted but to good fellowship and friendship. Warrington was full of warm hearte and the more often one warm heart met another, the better it would be for both of them and for Warrington. (Applause.) Having referred to the hippy way in which the citizens of Warrington fraternized, Mr. Smith said we did not know class wit. in Warrington. lie hoped we might never know it, but that any grievances under which the workingclaesee suffered would always be remedied without the preacO- ing of such a conflict. (Hear, beer.) He believed the men of Warrington, takinz them as a whole, had con- fidence in those who employed and bossed them, and that any differences that might crop up would be settled without having recourse to those violent and disistrous measures which, unfortunately, we bad omen throughout the country during the last few months. He believed that where they had the people of s town playing games and throwing themselves heart and soul into sport, they would also have a just community and one which was appreciative of all the best interests which it had to govern. The splendid epirit which prevailed amongst the people of Warrington was largely due to the fact that they were keen and enthusiastic sportsmen. ' (Applause). Mr. WILLErr supported the toast, lie said the association had had many ups and downs, but to-day it was one of the finest in the country. (Applause.) Tuey had attained that position by sheer hard work. (Applause). Mr. E. J. HALL proposed the health of the treasurer end secretary. He said it must be a 110UtC3 of satisfac- tion to the members that they had associated with them in those important offices, men who were so trustworthy and devoted to their interests. (Hoar, hear. is all departments of their lives, whether industrial, com- mercial, political or social, lidded Mr. Hall, the great signal to all of them was : Play the game and play it straight." LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

INSURANCE BILL DISCUSSED.

The National Insurance scheme propounded by Mr. Lloyd George was under dismission at the weekly meeting of the Warringtnn Parliamentary Debating Society in Cairo-street Church parlour on Tuesday evening. The Home Eittcnrranv (Councillor J. Hawthorn) introduced the following resolution :—" That this House welcomes the introduction into the House of Commons of the National Insurance Bill by the Chancellor of the Exchequer, believing that this great scheme of national insurance against invalidity and unemployment will lift the dead weight off the lives of an immense multitude of men and women in Great Britain, and trusts that the measure will soon become the law of the land." He said there might be some little differences of opinion as to the details of the Chancellor's great scheme, but be thought there was general agreement upon the main proposola The Bill constituted an attempt to set up for the first time in the history of this country a national health organization. Ci wrnany had led the way in a meneore of that kind and Mr. Lloyd George had profited by it. " Seeing that our friends on the other aide" commented the Home Secre- tary "are so fond of holding up Germany fur one adrairation, Ido not think they will now seriously object if we follow Germany in such a good cause as national insnraoce." (Laughter and appleuee.) That the question was an urgent one would be generally admitted. On the authority of the President of the Local Government Board, we had it that 20, per . cent, of th 3 pauperism in this country was attributable to sickness. To that we most Mao add another considerable percentage for ulemploye meet. The Old Age Pensions Act had proved that although the Poor Law authorities had power to deal with sickness and destitution, there were large numbers of the poor who would rather stiff, depriva- tion than wear the badge of pauperism. All honour to them. (Applause.) Praiseworthy efforts had been made by the working classes to insure against the ...emblem of life. There were three contingencies against which they insured : Death, sickness and unemployment. The insurance against death was pretty general and it was oomputed,that somethin like 42,000,000 policies were in existence. It was therefore not necessary for the Government to deal with that branch of the question. But in regerd to insurance against sickness and uaemplooment, the ground was not no well covered. Something like 6,000,000 people made some provision through the friendly societies against sickness, including provision for medical aid, and the trade unions cove -ed shout 1,500,000 workmen who had insured against unemploy- ment. That was far from adequate to meet the case. (Hear, hear). The main relsoa why more could not insure spinet these two wolves which were continually at the doors of the people Wan lack of funds. The wages of the workers were not sufficient to enable them to insure against all three contingencies without some assistance. It was that assistance which the Govern- ment now intended to give. (Applause.) The H >me Secretary then outlined the proposals of the Bill, and said it was expected that the number of people who would come under the invalidity section would total 14,700,000. The benefits would be dispensed through approved friendly societies which would be under the sole control of the members. Those nineties which were eentralized,such for instance as the Hearts of Oak, must form local committees of management; otherwise they would not distribute the benefits. Societies which already had large funds at their disposal would be at liberty to give additional benefits to those allowed in the insurance scheme, and in that way a healthy rivalry would exist for the large numbers who would shortly seek admittance. The trade anions would also he allowed to dispense benefits, if approved and of I suffirient numerical strength. There would be a value- tion of the funds of the friendly societies and those whoload impulses would be at liberty to offer aldi- I timid benefits. Then came the important quistion of the men and women who might be rejected by the approved societies or those who would not join a friendly society. It was proposed that they should be called Pont Office contributors. No encouragement was given under the Bill to that class of contributor, the object being ter strengthen the friendly societies and work through them. The benefits derived by Post Office contributors would not be quite at good as those who joined the friendly societies. Reference having been made to the proposed establishment of sanatoria@ for consumptives and th 3 formation 0; health authoritin to the vari?us the Home mecretary said it war estimated that in the first year of tha insurance invalidity scheme, the contributions wonld amount to £21,000,000 from the three sources, workpeople, employers and the State. The lint year's experience and benefits would he only £7,000,000. but that sum would have risen to £20,000,000 in 1915-16. The unemployment section of the Bill was next dealt with. It would at first only apply to two groups of trades—engineering and building —but it wan a great experiment that was well worth trying. The Insurance Bill celled for great sacrifices to contribute towards the defence of the homes of the country against the formidable and devastating invasion of disease. But he believed the gain to the national health would he great. It would save untold misery to masses of our fellow countrymen and women. The present methods of insuring against sickness unemployment were sporadic in their character inadequete in their purpose, and unstable in their work- ing. The Bill would, at any rate, give the contributors security that the money could be paid. Lard Beaconsfield saw forty years ego that national health was the foundation of notional efficiency. It had been left to the Chancellor of the Exchequer to promulgete a scheme to safeguard It. Of what avail was it to tell an outdoor worker who might be lying on a bed of. sickness that he lived in the richest country in the world, or that on the Empire of which we were so proud the sun never net, when all the time he knew that his wife and little ones were suffering the pangs of hunger through his inability to work. He annealed to the members of that House to help to relieve the worker of the dread which daily blunted him of being overcome by poverty through sickness and unemploy- ment. It the Empire was to fulfil het mission, it was essential that she should be wane to the core. If there was seething discontent amongst the workers there was trouble at the very heart of the Empire and unless it was removed, there would be social chaos and it would fall as other Empires had fallen. He believed the scheme, if given a fair chance, would relieve untold misery in numberless homes. It was social reform of the first order. (Applause.) Mr. G. Toebror; (L, Abingdon) seconded the adop- tion of the Bill. _ _

_ Mr. HARTLEY (C.. Skipton) said the Bill wog very good in theory, but when they reduced it to practice it had many flow.. Although he had every sympathy with the measure and believed it would ba of great advantage in many cases there were other cases in which it would mean injustice and some large classes of workmen would be considerably worse off than they were at present. He referred particularly to clerks. shop assist- ants and domestic servants. Is most well regulated offices it was the custom to pay the salaries of , clerks during illness—often for an indefinite ' period.linder Mr. Lloyd-George'. Bill there was not any arrangement whereby any firma which agreed to pay their clerks a minimum of six weeke wages (hying sickness would be allowed a rebate of Id„ per week from their contribution to the fund, but three would too i tendency to confine the allowance to the minimum period. At the present time the clerk paid nothing but under the Bill he would have to pay 3.1„ par w sek and ran the risk of receiving lees. The same applied to shop assiatants,and domestic servants. The speaker conten dad that the amount of benefits to be paid compared unfavourably with those of certain societies with which he had been connected. THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, NOVEMBER 11, 1911

The Balfour Must Go MR. BALFOUR party has had a swift GOES. and unexpected success.

Mr. BALFOUR has gone. He made his own announcement to this effect at a meeting of the City of London Con- servative Association on Wednesday. We cannot refrain from recalling the fact that, just before he was elected as member for the City of London, Mr. BALFOUR insisted on the necessity of his speedy return to the House of Commons. This was just after the great Conservative overthrow of 1906. The defeated leader thrown out of what was believed to be a perfectly safe seat at Manchester left no doubt about his own views of his political importance. He believed that his party would be lost without him, and we perceive no grounds for thinking that he was mistaken on that subject. He has, since hiR return by the City, maintained a remarkably steady, persistent, and often very brilliant fight against the Government. But all through the time he has had to contend with disloyal groups in his own party. The " B. M. G." movement is only the latest of similar uprisings against Mr. BALFOUR'S leadership. It is not for us to say that any one of them was without justifi- cation. But we are quite clear about one thing. All the movements arose out of a dissatisfaction which gave no thought to Mr. BALFoua's successor He may not have been the ideal leader of a party overtaken by disaster, but he was the only discoverable leader in the Conservative ranks. His resignation of the leadership places the Opposition in a situation which is so open to ridicule that generous opponents must be inclined to conceal their real feelings. When Mr. BALFOUR is got rid of, what remains? That is to say, now that the " 8.M.G." party has enforced on the Con- servative leader a line of conduct that he has obviously been unwilling to take, what will be the next step? There is a generally recognised rule in regard to an Opposition which turns out a Government. This is that the Opposition must itself be prepared to accept the onerous responsibilities of power. The rule applies to the leadership of parties. Should a leader be hustled out of his leader- ship—which is something that has not occurred within living memory—there must be, or at least there should be, some deter- mination as to his successor. It does not appear to us that the Tory party contains any successor to Mr. BALFOUR, except in the sense that Mr. W. H. SMITH was a successor, let us say to such a leader of the House of Commons as Mr DISRAELI. Mr. Slant was the most admirable stop-gap ever known. He said nothing. He did nothing. He presented an impregnable front. Under existing circumstances it is not possible to find on the Conservative benches a member of such high authority and noble powers of silence and resistance as Mr. W. H. SMITH. What is to be found there? In the House of Lords, of course, the MARQUIS OF LANSDOWNE. He has declared that he will stand or fall by Mr. BALFOUR, but we shall take the liberty of believing that Lord LartsoowNE's declaration has a limited interpretation. It will prove to

mean that he will stand by Mr. BALFOUR'S views, quite independently of Mr. BALFOUR'S action. He will not retire as Mr. BALFOUR has done, but come more absolutely to the front as the leader of the party. Then as to the House of Commons, there is a not very extensive choice for the leadership. The B. M. G." men are out of the reckoning. The choice of the party must lie between Mr. WALTER LONG, Mr. AUSTEN CHAMBERLAIN, and Mr. BONAR LAW. We need have no hesitation in saying that Mr. WALTER LONG will be the NV. H. Smith of the occasion. But will Mr. BALFOUR go after all? We think not. He is tired, and he is not very well. Physically he has never been strong, and if there is anything catcbing about the House of Commons he always catches it. He needs rest, no doubt. But we incline to believe that what tires him most,andiwbat he most abhors,is the attacks from his own party to which he has recently been exposed. It is from these that he wants rest. They cannot be described as pinpricks. They have been bludgeonings. He has now taken the only step consonant with honour and self-respect. But it would surprise us very much if Mr. BALFOUR were to cease to be the leader of the Conservative party. There will be h meeting of the party at large. The Halsbury Club men and the "B. M. G." men will be as far outnumbered as is reasonable in a Parlia- mentary party which is not great in numbers. Mr. BALFOUR will be offered as much time as he desires for recruiting his health, but the party leadership will be fixed on his brow as if it were a crown of wild olive. He will be led back to the leadership in floral garlands. The "B. M. G." party will disappear with a suddenness and a complete- ness that history will not have leisure to note.

At a later date we THE REV. JOHN shall have the oppor- YONGE. tunity of referring more adequately to the cessation of the Rev. John Yonge's minis- terial labours in the town. But we cannot let the announcement of his impending resig- nation from the pastorat of Wycliffe Con- gregational Church pass without saying how much we regret that the inexorable law of time has forced Mr. Yonge to contemplate a retirement into a less prominent sphere of activity. After half a century of ministerial labour, however, it is too much to expect that continuous sacrifice of bodily and mental energy in the highest of earthly vocations even from such a whole-hearted "labourer in the vineyard" as Mr. Yonge. He has played no inconsiderable part in the life of the town, and the Wycliffe pulpit, during his long occupancy of it, has always been a fountain from which has issued a clear stream of practical Christianity. We are sure that both Mr. and Mrs. Yonge will take with them into their ultimate retirement the warmest regard of a community so wide that its limits none can define.

Our congratulations are THE NEW FREEMAN. respectfully offered to Mr. Robert Pierpoint on the signal honour conferred upon him by the municipality on Thursday. In adding his name to the select list of the Honorary Free- men of the Bor augh,the Corporation have con- ferred honour where honour is due, and we hope made easier the impending severance of Mr. and Mrs. Pierpoint from the town with which their association has been so close and so dear. Mr. Pierpoint had to sit through a swelling chorus of eulogy—always a trial to a modest man—and in his response he touched quite the right note of dignity and personal appreciation of the honour conferred upon his family and forbears equally with himself. Later in the day, at the ,Mayoral banquet, he uttered a "last word of peculiar import and significance. Touching on the break in his representation of the Borough brought about by Mr. Crosfield's double success in 1906 and 1910, Mr. Pierpoint said he had " not lost one day or one working hour without a consuming desire to get back to his place. It may be, for who knows what is on the knees of the gods, that I shall get back there agaia. I hope that the time may come." This, if we may say so, is a curious and very possibly pregnant utterance by the ex-Conservative leader. It may ba that some other constituency would be glad to avail itself of Mr. Pierpoint's Parliamentary experience and willingness to serve, but we cannot help thinking that what he said had peculiar reference to Warrington,and the combination of strange circumstances which brought about the position of isolation which the speaker unwillingly occupies. While we cannot express the hop 3 that Warrington will ever again trust its Parlia- mentary representation to Mr. Pierpoint, or any other Conservative, we should be very gladi to see Mr. Pierpoint once more the chosen standard-bearer of his party. He was a worthy foe whether in victory or defeat, and one's happiest recollections of political warfare are associated with the time when Robert Pierpoint opposed the chosen nominee of Liberalism and Progress.

We are loth to pursue WHAT ABOUT our National Service THE BAND? League friends with any seeming relentless- ness, but instead of making the amende honorable to the Champion Band they have aggravated and repeated the offence for which we criticised them a fortnight ago. The current issue of the Leagues official journal" The Nation in Arms,' published this week, contains glowing accounts of the meeting in Parr Hall, and the over-flow meeting, and again there is the serious omission of all reference to the presence of the Champion Band. The League greedily claims all the credit for the great audience ' and intentionally misleads its own friends. Perpend : Warrington, October 18—Annual meeting. Over 2,400 present in packed hall. . . . The attendance was so enormous that an overflow meeting in the next largest hall was hastily arranged as follows. Warrington October 18—Overibw meeting : 700 present. . . . The result of this sweep- ing success has been to bring many " wobblers ' over to our side.

How envious the struggling officials of other branches of the League in similar towns must feel on reading the above in their official journal. They must think that Warrington has gone over to compulsory military training "lock, stock, and barrel" —and marvel thereat. They do not know, poor deluded ones, that the Champion Band of England, fresh from a Crystal Palace -triumph, was the great attraction. And they cannot possibly guess that at the " hastily arranged" overflow meeting there was displayed a placard (no doubt equally hastily printed) announcing that "the Champion Band will play." It would be a

[shock, no doubt, to the innocent readers of "The Nation in Arms' to learn that 90 per cent, of these 3,100 people who flocked to the National Service League meeting on this memorable occasion were present solely to hear the band which has been so curiously overlooked in the circulated reports, and showed marked impatience with the pro- ceedings until Bandmaster Smith came to the front and took up his baton ! We cannot compliment the National Service League on anything but the ingenuity of its methods of "bringing over" the " wobblers."

In the course of the

WHO PAYS? controversy started at the meeting of share- holders in Pears, Limited, Mr. Barratt, the chairman of the board, asserted roundly 1 that the American people paid every farthing of the £400,000 levied by the Custom House in America on his company's goods. On this he was challenged by Mr. Cooper, M.P., to say how, in that case, could the building of a factory in America be of any advantage to his firm. There is no want of completeness in the overwhelming reply from Mr. Barratt, which appeared in the Daily News." He writes: The wholesome price of a certain univer- sally known speciality of ours—which snail be entirely nameless(et ex uno disce omnes) 1 —is 69. sd. per dozen in England and 109. per dozen in America,the difference being duty, freight, and other expenses paid by the con- sumer ; the exact net amount brought back to us in England being again 6s. sd. Who paid the duty? Now, if we can make in America and put some of the duty now paid by Americans into our own pockets—should such duties 1 continue—l assume that Mr. Cooper will

consider us justified in doing so. Tne American consumer paid exactly thirty-seven pence—or over 44 per cent.— in excess of the Free Trade price. We hope that is clear eno-agh.

It sometimes happens MANHOOD that the boldest solu- SUFFRAGE. tion of a problem is really the easiest. Certainly that is the case in regard to the franchise problem. The plural vote is an absurdity which has long been crying out for treatment. The latchkey vote, which has been the sport of conflicting decisions in the Law Courts, called no less urgently for immediate treatment. But even when these flagrant anomalies had been removed, there would remain a complicated, ridiculous, and inequitable franchise system. The states- manlike course is obviously that which Mr. Asquith has decided to take, though its apparent boldness has made slow- minded observers gasp. The existing 'franchise system may be interesting to the antiquarian as a monument to the strength of privilege in this country in the past. But it has no justification in reason or practice. The plural vote has been condemned in principle by the Tories themselves, even while they shelter behind it. How can they defend the plural vote at elections when they pro- fess to desire a Referendum for which it will be eliminated on the most vital questions? The whole artificial distinctions bAween different categories of franchise may be cherished by the Tory Party as obstacles to a really democratic franchise, but there is no honest plea which can be put forward in their behalf, even from the Tory point of view, except that they are undemocratic; that they lend themselves to cunning manipulation by a Party which, in advocat- ing the Referendum, has pledged itself, nominally, to an absolutely democratic prin- ciple. Finally, manhood suffrage has been accepted by the self-governing colonies. The " first plank" in the Tory "constructive programme" is declared to be an arrange- ment which will draw the colonies nearer to Ourselves. It will be interesting to see how far the Tories can go in denouncing as revolutionary a suffrage which will bring US into closer sympathy with the political tendencies of the self-governing colonies.

- One of the most interesting articles in the November " Munsey " is that on "The Beecher Family." The moral character of John Wesley and that of Henry Ward Beecher were attacked by enemies on pre- cisely the same ground, but the integrity of both men is so fully established that the world has forgotton the assaults upon Wesley, and it will soon forget the bitter foes who assailed Beecher. The time was when he was perhaps the best-hated man in , the world, by reason of his attack on slavery and secession, but he lived to receive tumultuous welcome and applause in Rich- mond and Atlanta. When the War Secretary recommended that they should ask Beecher to lift the old flag back to its place on Fort Sumter, Lincoln said: " But for Beecher's speeches in England, there might have been no flag to raise!"

Towards the end of his life Mr. Glact*,- •plsepd the creation of the Post Office Saville; Bank third among the achievements of his life. The idea of the bank is one which has borne abundant fruit. At the celebration of the jubilee of its foundation the other night Mr. Lloyd George was able to say that the deposits at the end of the present year will be over £170,000,000. As a rule we are not a thrifty people, but the accumulation of a sum which amounts to £4 a head of the population is evidence at once of the popu- larity of this method of saving, and of a growing care for the morrow * * *

• Councillor Dr. Joseph has entered upon a second year as Mayor of Warrington with the fullest confidence and support of the whole of the townspeople. The members of the Council competed with each other on Thursday in their eagerness to express good-will and loyalty to His Worship, and Alderman Tinnion made an oblique, but appropriate reference to the "added responsibilities" which His Worship was about to undertake in the new year. We are not sure that the Alderman put what is a purely personal matter in the best light, though he had every intention of doing so, but we venture to offer our warmest con- gratulations and best wishes to the Mayor and to tin future now Mayoress of Warringtot. * *

To the list of distinguished people who have honoured Warrington with visits through the enterprise of the Philomathic Society must now be added Miss Ellen Terry. This greatest of actresses would seem to be endowed with perennial youth, and the large audience which she held enraptured by her Shakespearean recital found it hard to realize that Miss Terry has indeed celebrated her jubilee on the stage. A delightful feature of her studies of Shakes- peare's triumphant women was the happily worded commentaries, with present-day applications, which she contrived to intro- duce. In speaking of Portia she had something to say of the modern advocates of woman's rights who are inclined to regard the heroine's surrender to her lord, Bassanio as altogether too abject and servile. By clever and convincing reasoning Miss Terry put quite a different construction on the case and made a point of the fact that Portia resolved upon her great part in the trial without so much as a by your leave" to her husband. Another delightful touch was Miss Terry's conclusion that the scheme for Shylock's overthrow was ' so ingenious that only a woman could have I thought of it ! • • •

Doubt no longer exists that the year 1911 is going to put our trade figures at a height they have never before reached. In spite of the temporary checks of strikes and disputes the advance goes on amazingly. The October figures almost take the breath away. There is five and a quarter millions increase in British exports and two and three-quarter millions increase in imports. Over the ten months of the year exports have risen by £18,500,000, and imports by £5,000,000. These are the results after which the soul of the Tariff Reformer hankers, but they are pro- duced by our system of Free Trade. Four and three-quarter millions of the increase in exports in October is in manufactured goods, and only £BOO,OOO more of manufactured goods have come from abroad. Figures such as these speak of an amazing prosperity. The improvement in exports, however, must be due in considerable part to increased invest- ment abroad. If we did not believe (says the "Westminster Gazette),-that we should have to regard the disproportionate increase as an evil rather than a good. As it is the balance will be made more even as the interest upon these investments begins to flow homeward.

* The mass of routine business al ways associ- ated with the annual meeting of the Town Council was transacted with incredible rapidity on Thursday. Of late years long and tiresome discussions have waged round such apparently innocent proposals as the confirmation of Standing Orders and the appointment of committees. This time, however, all was different, the resolu- tions being passed with a clockwork precision which suggested that everything had been "cut and dried." In the past it has ' been the custom to appoint each committee separately but on the present occasion they were elected en bloc in less than five seconds. To most of the members, the procedure was a welcome innovation, in view of the fact that nearly an hour end a half had been monopolized by the election of Mayor and the presentation of the Freedom of the borough to Mr. Robert Pierpoint—two ceremonies which called forth all the oratorical resources of the Council.Councillor Pemberton, however, seemed exceeding wrathful at the sudden termination of the meeting. Apparently he had intended to raise some controversial point but had, for once, been caught napping in the speed at which the tail-end of the agenda was rushed through.

Lecture by Rev. Prebendary Wakeford.

A lecture outland " Marriage and ll.vorce " wai delivered by the Rev. Prebendary Wake!. rd, vicar of St. Margaret's, Anfield, Liverpool, at St. Clement's Mission last evening, under the auspices of the War- rington Church Society.—The Lecturer said that while legislation affected morality he was not prepared to say that legislation could make a moral rule. It could contribute greatly to the morality and immoraLty of people. The State went towards dissolving marriage altogether by allowing divorce, and offered a suggestion that marriage WIL9 not a binding contract thus leading to the most calamitous results in social life. Severe Gale. Warrington and district felt the full force of the gale which swept the country on Sunday. The velocity of the wind at times was estimated at approaching 90 , miles an hour, the average being no less than 00 miles 'an b or. Warrington was, however, fortunate in escaping serious damage, although property suffered to some extent. The sign of the Saracen's Head Hotel was torn from Its posit:on and thrown ' into the adjoining shrubbery, while slates and tiles were to be aces flying about in some streets, several narrow escapes being witnessed. There was an exceptionally high tide in the Mersey and at the junction of Chester-road and Ricer-road the river overflowed its banks. The water in the old river, running parallel with Wilderepool-causeway, also reached such an unusual height that the spray dashed over tits footpath and the iron rails at the Stockton Heath end into the adjoining field.

Esperanto Social. On Wednesday evening a very successful gathering of EsperantiSte took place in the League of the Cross Hall, Bewsey-street, where the local club meets every Wednesday. Contingents of Esperantists came from Liverpool, Manchester, Chester, Stockport, Southport Leigh, Golborne, St. Helens, Widnes. During the evening two rousing choruses were sung " L'Eapero " (the -International Anthem) and" Al la Esperantistaro" (to the tune cf " Men of Harlech.") Miss Griffin of St Helens sang " 0 kuloj du "(Two Eyes of Grey] very tastefully, while Mr. Fred West of St. Helens was very happy in his rendering of the " Bandolero." Mr. James Caldwell's singing of "Thora " was much appreciated. The remainder of the evening was devoted to dancing and conversation in Esperanto by those who love to hear the musical cadences of the "Ram lingvo." Everyone commented on the splendid feeling of goodfellowship and sociability among strangers drawn together from so many aifferent centres but one and all imbued with the true spirit of Esperantiam.

Bold-street Wesleyan Band of Hope. On Wednesday evening a very interesting and enjoyable lantern lecture on" Wild nature won by kindness" was given in the Bold-street schoolroom by Mr. Et. E. O'Callaghan, of London. The Rev. H. Williamson pesided over a large grthering of young people, who thoroughly enjoyed seeing the beautiful pictures and listening to the interesting stories,which showed the marvellous results of kindness, on even the savage beasts. The lecturer urged the children to be kind not only to their friends and neighbours, but to all dumb animals and so help forward that happy time of peace spoken of by the Prophet Isaiah. Duriag the evening the Band of Hope children sang " Open the door for the children " with pleasing effect. A recitation was given by Maggie Hardy. " Shall I ever be a drunkard " ; Eva Alcock and Sophia Cross sang very nicely a duet entitled " There's somebody else that will." A spirited dialogue was rendered by Alice Nurse and others, "The Teetotal Dolls." Two songs were sweetly sung by Elsie Dawson and Cynthia Dawson, " The Children's Home "and " Annie Laurie." Miss Annie Lawton was the accompanist A very hearty vote of thanks was given to Mr. O'Callaghan and all who had taken part in the proceedings. Mr. David Cleges Visit. . .

Mr. David Clegg, one of the most popular organists of the day, pad another visit to Warrington on Saturday evening and gave a recital in Boldatreet Wesleyan Church. Mr. Clegg's reputation as a brilliant executant was sufficient to attract a large gathering and a well-varied programme was followed with great appreciation. As usual the selections were ' of a kind calculated to appeal to a general audience, and included orchestral and military band pieces, as well as the wonderfully vivid representation of The Storm." Mr. Clegg possesses a thorough mastery of the resources of" the king if instruments," and is able to produce a surprising wealth of tone colour and many unique effects. Bis technique is wonderfully good and, whatever may be eh, unlit of the unconventional methods be employs, his recitals are always a pleasure to listen to.—Warrinzton people will sympathise with Mr. Clegg in the misfortune which befel him during the gale on Sunday. The roof of the Fariholme Skating Rink, Bacup, was blawn in, and Mr. Cleg,g's newly invented great band organ. costing £2,000, which had been erected in the building, was destroyed. Mr. Clegg arm toning the organ, aid had a marvellous escape, masonry and debris smashing the instrument to atoms. Sacred performances were to have been given by Mr. Clegg on the organ, which represents one hundred performers.

St. Peters Men's Class Social. A social evening was held under the anspicPs of the above class on Monday evening last, November 6th, in the schools in John street, when a large number of members and their friends assembled and spent a pleasant evening. A capital programme was gone through the artistes being Mr. Sam Aukland, who created much amusement with hid songs and sketches, Mr White who sang with great effect "Mary of Argylle" and' My Sweetheart when a boy," and Mrs Monks who was encored for her singing of " Hearts" and " Flight of Ages." An int-resting event took place daring the interval for refreshment@ when the Vicar (Bev. B. Percival), presented Mr. James Taylor with a silver mounted walking stick as a souvenir from the class on his retirement from the secretaryship after 16 years service. In a humorous speech the Vicar spoke very eulogistically of Mr. Taylor and said he hoped he would take that present as showing the good esteem in which he was held. Mr. Taylor suitably responded. Further singing and dancing was indulged in until 10.45 when, after a vote of thanks proposed by the Rev. ,l. H. Garnett to the workers and committee, a very pleasant evening was brought to a close. RESIGNATION OF MR. BALFOUR.

DRAMATIC RETIREMENT FROM THE PARTY LEADERSHIP.

Who Will Succeed Him?

With dramatic and startling unexpectedness XI r. Balfour on Wednesday announced, at a meeting of the executive of the City Conservative Association, his resignation of the leadership of the party. The meeting had been cared only on the previous day, and most of thrse present had no suspicion of what was about to hapreni

In a dignified and in many respects pathetic speech Mr. Balfour announced his retirement on grounds of health and the increasing demands of Parliamentary life. I desire (he said to leave active life before I can be suspected of suffering from the most insidious of all diseases, the disease which comes upon those who, without losing their health or their intellect, nevertheless get somewhat petrified in the old course they have pursued, whose authority grows because they have been long in the public service, and who cannot deal with the r.ew problems which in this changing world are perpetually arising, with the freshness and elasticity which are desirable. lam vain enough to hope that I have not reached that period, but I should be miserable if I ran the margin too fine, and nothing, I think, would be more terrible to realise than that while Female were looking to you more than ever for leadership, owing to your lengthening experience you have not the keenness or the alertness— which most go with increasing years—adequately to meet the demands made upon you. I am confident that I could not, if the fortunes of our party were to, as I think they will, rise to their ancient height, I have not I am confident, . . .

the vigour again to conduct a Ministry. He would, added Mr. Balfour, continue to remain in Parliament. In conclusioa he appealed for loyalty to his successor. . . . .

The news was received by the bulk of the Con- ssrvative party with consternation, though the " 8.M.51." section are no doubt satisfied. Mr. Balfour was to have attended a dinner on Wednesday night at the Savoy Hotel to Mr. Hayes Fisher. M.P , given by the Municipal Reform party. The following statement, however, was read from bin The statement to my constitutents this after- noon provides. I hope, an adequate reason for my absenting myself from the banquet where I had hoped to pry a personal tribute to my old friend and colleague. Mr. Hayes Fisher. Circumstances, however, made it necessary to anticipate the date at which I bad originally designed to announce my registration of the leadership of tire party, or I would have given you timely notice of the fact that I was unal,le to be present with you to-night

7

I Curiosity will certainly be aroused as to what are the " circumstances " referred to.

The retiring lender, vrho is C,3 years old, and hae been 3i years in Parliament, had an audience of the King during the day at Buckingham Palace. A meeting of Conservative M.P.,'" will be held next week, when Mr. Walter Long or kfr."Austen Chamber- lain will probably be chosen to lead the party in the Commons. Lord Lansdowne will still lead the Lords.

MR. A. H. CROSFIELD INTERVIEWED. GENEROUS TRIBUTE TO MR. BALFOUR. Forecast of His Successor.

What do you think about the news of Mr. Balfour's retirement from the leadership?" asked as " Examiner" representative who had a short chat with Hr. A. H. Crostield on Thursday evening with refer- ence to the latest startling developmeut in the political situation.

" Why, I suppose as a party man I ought to feel glad," teplied Crostield. " But I don't. I noticed • few weeks since that an old friend of mine criticised me in the "Examiner" for what I think he termed flattery of Mr. Balfour. Now, ever since I began to take an active part in polities I hare found myself utterly opposed, time after time, to Mr. Balfour's policy and fighting tooth and nail sgainbt it. But that has never blinded me to the qualities of tLe man himself. Mr. BalfonrLt intellectual eminence is, of course, well known through- out the country, but those who have sat with him in the House of Commons can best appreciate on the one hand his personal charm and on the other the superb courage with which he has fought his battle ma • position that seemed at times almost impossible " " You think the • Die Hartle ' have been trytug to force Mr. Balfour's hand ?"

" I have not a shadow of a doubt of it—indeed, we have bad plenty of public evidence of that tact. I believe Mr. Balfour has regarded it as part of his mission, while saving the Conservative party a split from end to end of it, tn save his party and the country from the grosser ..nd cruder Protectionist proposals of the ' Die Herds.' Mr. Balfour would, I have no doubt, be quite willing to see a large number of small duties placed on a large number of articles, although that would mean introducing to our public life the corruption that follows in the wake of Protection, for the sake of a revenue that would barely pay for the coot of collecting it. But in spite of the pressure put upon him by Mr. Chamberlain and his followers, Mr. Balfour has steadily refused to lend himself to the gross and preposterous fiscal clap-trap with which your full-blooded Protectionists are, day in and day out, seeking to gull the country. He has succeeded in keeping his end up and has fought his battle in the country and in the House with the greatest fortitude and skill." „ •

"You rate lus abilities as a debater very high?" "I have always regarded Mr. Balfour as head and shoulders above any other man in his party ; and I don't envy the task of anyone who tries to lead it with Mr. Balfour sitting beside him ia the House." 'Who, do you think, will be his successor?" "If you mesh in the leadership of the Conservative party, there can be no doubt, I think, that the forecast by common consent of the Press to-day will prove correct and that Lord Lansdowne will become the leader of the party. But if you refer to the Howe of Commons, I certainly expect to see that Mr. Welter Long will be elected. He is one of thoee men who is universally liked and respected in the House—every inch a gentleman and not deficient in debating power. But, of course, Mr. Walter Long has not the faintest pretension to the brilliant intelh-ct and experienced statesmanship of Mr. Balfour." "But what about the other men who have been talked about as possible leaders of the Tory Party ? " " Well " said Mr. Crosfield with a laugh, "You saw the hint given them by Mr. Walter Long when he was speaking in Lancashire the othir day and remarked that there were rather too many of these would-be leaders. Among a large section of the Conservative party, hicluding I may say the most influential section, the names of certain aspirants are received with nothing less than a shudder, and well I can imagine the dismay with which the great majority of Conservatives will contrast the would-be leaders -I refer to with a statesman of eminence and distinction like Mr. Balfour. I personally, having made his acquaintance years before I entered the House of Commons, shall always cherish as one of my pleasantest recollections of the House, the kiudnese with which he met one there when I entered it as one of his po,itical opponents and the many interesting and pleasant talks I had with him. But then, that is only characteristic of the courtesy with which Mr. Balfour has treated supporters and opponents alike in the House."

" One more questios, Mr. Crosfield. What do ycu think of the outlook f"

" Well,! cannot help feeling that, whatever the nominal change of leadership may be, it is to Mr. Balfour that members of the House of Commons and the country 'generally will continue to look, as the chief spokesman of Conservative opinion."

In Australia they hal a Church Lnion, in con- nection with which all sor:s of competitions were ran all the year round, and they had in that way got one of the strongest competitions of any be had beard. Canon Stevenson on Sunday School Work. Canon Morley Stevenson, of Warrington, in the course of a lecture on Sundiy school work at St. Paul's Parochial Hall, Widnes, said that order was heaven's first law, and if there was a place in which good order should be found it was a Sunday school. Yet it was common to find good order not maintained. All teachers were not born to command, but all, by thought, I tact and care could succeed in miintaining the interest and attention of the children. The qualifications of an ideal teacher were simply those of an idsal person. All the teacher said or did should be as near perfect as was possible, for he Wino the example ever before the child. An effort was boing made in some Sunday schools by means of models and illustrations to hold more securely the attention of the children. Ile thought money used in this way was more advantageous than it spent on prises. Teachers should train themselves in narrating stories and repre- senting characters as life-like and as bright as possible "A teacher must blaze himself in order to make his children burn," someone had said, and he believed that the spark most be In the teacher before the children would catch fire.

Hill Cliffs Baptist Church.

Recognltloo of the Rey. G. Curtis.

The Rev George Curtis, who recently took up the pastorship of the Hill Cliffs Baptist Church, was , welcomed at a special recognition services held on ' Thursday last. In the afternoon a sermon was preached by the Rev. W. Owen who took his test from I Matthew xvii., 19, " When the devil cometh we must cast him out." He said that the diniples of old and the evil spirit were typical of the present day Christians and am. He referred to the failure of the disciples in the casting out of the evil spirit and said that the Christians were a reflection of the failure in their endeavour to cast out sin, lie emphasised the faiit that work and worship which always went together, should be com- bined, as that was the way to the winning of souls. At the service in the evening there was a very large I congregation. The chair was occupied by Mr. W. IH. Bradfor,'. (in the absence of Dr. Hitcbena. of Heywood), who WU supported by the pastor (the Rev. Geo. Curtis), Revs. W. Owen, Silas Rixon, W. S. Llewellyn, D. 0. Davie., 11. Ogde3 and .I. S. Chedburn. He hoped their church would witness a groat revival and that through his work lustre would be added to its fine historical record. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN blid this yen had witnessed some of the most stirring events that had ever happened in the country—the Coronation, the railway strike, which had been such a detriment to the trade of the country, the exceedingly hot and dry summer which would be remembered for a considerable time by the farmera, and lastly the event of the previous day, the resignation of Mr. Balfour. THE RE Vi JOHN YONGE.

Impending Resignation after 50 Years in the Ministry.

A momentous announcement, and one which fell on sympathettc ears, was made by the Rev. John Yonge (pastor), to the congregation at the evening service in Wycliffe Congregational Church, on Sunday. Mr. Yonge, in the course of a gracefully worded and impressive statement, informed the congregation that that teas the 35th anniversary of his settlement among them as pastor, and he thought the time was at hand when a younger man should be appointed to succeed him. Hs would next year complete 50 years' eervice in the ministry. That was a privilege which was not vouchsafed to many ministers, and, if spared until then, it would be his desire to be relieved of his duties as pastor of that church. Naturally there were expressions of deep regret at the approJching severance of such a long and notable connection with the church, but file hope was expressed that the evening of the days of both the reverend gentleman and Mis. Yonge would b 3 full or light and peace. The Rev. J ho Yonge is a veteran Congregationaliat. In 1866 he settled at Heywood, and his ministry, a successful one, extended over a period of ten years. On the sth November, 1876, he succeeded the late Rev. George Sale Heaney no pastor of the Wycliffe Church in Bewsey-street. He was quite a stranger to the town, but kince that time he hart, by his ability, consistency and great devotion to duty, won the love and respect of the people generally. He was educated at Cheshunt College. For many years he has acted as secretary to the Liverpool district of the Lancashire Congregational Union, and an evidence of the great appreciation in which he is held by his brethren in the ministry is afforded by the honour conferred upon him in 1890, when he was elected to the presidency of the County Union. He has alto been president of the Congregational Boards of Manchester and Liverpool. His son, Mr. J. A. Yonge is headmaster of Silcoates School, Wakefield. The Rev. John Yonge has thrown himself heart and sold into every good movement inaugurated in We rrington—social, educational and religious. He has for many years been a conspicuous member of the Temperance Union and an active member of the Infirmary Board of Management. He is a very successful preacher and most of the burning questions if the day have been fearlessly, exhaustively and ably treated by him from the pulpit. The probabilities are that the reverend gentleman will terminate his pastorate at Wycliffe abut Midsummer.

Parish Church Sale of Work.

The three-days' sale of work in connection with the Parish Church, was brought to a close on Saturday. The opening ceremony woo performed by the Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph), who was accompanied by the Mayoress (Miss Joseph). There was a large attendance, the Rector, the Rev. Canon Willis presiding. The RECTOR announced that the proceeds for the previoua day amounted to £lO4 18a. 84. than bring- lug the total for the two days to .C 205. (Applause). The objects of the sale were threefold. The flrst was the , Sunday school. He could never feel too grateful to I the education authorities for all they had done to improve the appeararce of the school. When they I looked around they could see a vast difference, and how ! much more attractive it was as compared with a few I years ago. From soother point of view however, he I did not feel satisfied. He felt thankful for the religious instiuction in the elementary schools but he was greatly distressed to think that there was no such teaching in the Secondary school. As the education authority had granted the principle of religious , instruction in the elementary schools he could not see I why they should not have gone a stop farther and have granted it to the Secondary school. In the meantime he hoped that the parents would realise the importance of their duty in this matter. Thee should do all in their power to elect men on the Council who had the matter at heart and would do their utmost for its furtherance. Another duty of the parent was to take advantage of the classes fort e teaching of religious instruction out of school he .irs, by seeing that their I children attended. The second object of the sale was to assist the nick and poor fund. 'they gave about £lOO a year to the fund but he thought that was insufficient and he hoped that sale would enable them to give a much larger contribution. Then came the Church Lads' Brigade, as the third object of the sale. That institu- tion was a most important one, and they had good reason to be thankful for the services of the boys on several occasions. The Rector warmly congratulated the Mayor upon his decision to take office for a second ye•r. The MAYOR in declaring the sale open, said the objects of the sale of work should commend themselves to everyone. The religious, nodal, moral and last but not least the sanitary progress cf the people were of paramount importance and it was our duty to encourage that progress. The power and success of an empire or country was not to be judged by the wealth or the wealthy people it contained but by the number of hrppy and contented people it possessed. He thought Warrington would compare favourably with any town in that respect, and he had often heard that feeling expressed in different parts of the country. Ile thought the objects for which that sale was being held were all excellent objects and well worthy of the best efforts of them all to ensure success. (Applause.) The MAYORESS also expressed a hope that their efforts would be crowned with succeas. Councillor J. T. LOCKER, in proposing a vote of thanks to the Mayor, said that he could claim hiore accoaintance with the Mayor since he had held that positinn, and during that time he was sure he was voicing the thoughts of all Warringtonians when he said that the Mayor was one of the best friends that the town had ever possessed. Ile also thanked the Mayoress for her presence. Mr. G. Bnownc, in seconding, said that be could only echo the words of Councillor Locker as to their delight at the Mayor and Mayoress' presence. He (the speaker) sometimes wondered how they found time to attend so many events in such a large county borough. The MAYOR briefly responded. The total receipts for the whole sale amounted to £287 Bs. 7d.

Lectures to Sunday School Teachers Faults of the Sunday Schools. . .

The first of a series of lectures to Sunday school teachers was given in St. Pouts school on Tuesday even- ing, when the Rev. J. H. Bankes spoke of " The need of reform in our Sunday schools." Among the clergy present were the Rector (Rev. Canon Willis). Revs. W. Bracecamp, E. E. Peters, and the Rev. J M. Buckroaster. The Rev. J. H. BANKES said Sunday schools were very much behind the times—fifty or sixty years behind the ordinary day school. Dealing with the faults of the system be said some teachers went to school totally unprepared and they resorted to the terribla practice of preaching the Bible verse by verse in the class. Again, the majority of the teachers knew very little about the child ; how could they teach them if they did not understand child nature? (Hear, hear.) The classes in the schools were too large and the number of teachers inadequate. This state of things most be swept away ; the scholar and not the Bible should be the main object. (Applause.) A teacher , could not be judged by what his scholars knew but by their character. So to reform the schools, first of al

RECITAL.

" There is sweet music here that eofter falls Than petals of blown roses on the grass ; Or night dews on still waters between walls Of shadowy granite in a gleaming pans Music t bat gen tl ier on the spirit lies Than tired eyelids on tired eyes." NEW POST OFFICE MONEY BOXES.

With a view to encouraging thrift among depositors of the Post Office Savings Bank the Postmaster-General has arranged to supply money-boxes. These are made of highly. polished steel and weigh about tcn ounces. Every depositor will be able to obtain one of these neat but impregnable little boxes for the sum of is. as registration fee, and a deposit of 25., which latter amount will be refunded when the box is returned in good Condition. They are to be issued next month.

ROMAN BRITAIN.

During the last few years the Manchester and District Branch of the Claneical Association has be conducting the excavation, originally begun by Professor Gersten& of Liverpool University, and Mr. Thorns. May, of Warrington, on the site of the Roman fort at itibcheater. More than three years having now elapsed since the close of their last excsvation on the site, the Eacaystion Committee have deemed it derirable, in issuing on appeal for funds, to publish a brief statement of the Present position of the work at Itibohester. The pamphlet in which form the statement is presented is being issued to the members of the association in plac 3 of an annual report. The work at Ribcheater has resulted, it is stated, in Ands of greet interest. The foundations of part of the outer wall of the camp sod of one of the four greet gateways have been uncovered, and two large stonsbuilt buildings which had apparently as the granarie, of the camp have been partially cleared. Among the objects found special importance attaches to a fragmental./ inscription containing refer- ences to the Emperors Severii3 and Camelia, and to two carved Stone capitals of unusual size and elaboration.

TORY'S DISLIKE OF THE REFERENDUNL

The Hon, J. C. Lyttelton, M.P., opeakirg at Kidder- minster on Saturdsy, said frankly he did not like the referendum. To introduce the referendum would be to introduce a more novel feature into political life than bed ,yet been introduced, would absolutely upset all the traditions that the Houses of Parliament had behind them, and would take all responsibility from the Government, besides being expensive and leaning to a great deal of trouble.

===== 0002601_19111111_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM ' LABOUR'S STANDPOINT. ~......N.-,,,---,..,,-,...-...,3

By Philip Snowden, M.P.

THE UNEMPLOYMENT INSURANCE.

The Unemployment part of the Insurance Bill has been sent to a Grand Committee which has just begun to consider the measure in detail. During the six months the Bill has been before the country hardly anything at all has been heard about this section of it. The Chancellor of the Exchequer has kept his Sickness Insurance scheme so much to the front that Mr. Buxton, who is in charge of the Unemployment section, has been hidden from public view. Mr. Buxton has addressed no great public meetings in support of his part of the Bill, nor has he interviewed 100 deputations, nor written 2,500 explana- tory letters. The trade unions have been singularly quiet about this Unemployment Insurance scheme, and on the whole it may be said that there is neither interest nor knowledge prevailing about it. AN IMPORTANT SCHEME. But this Unemployment Insurance is by no means the least important part of the Bill, and it is well that the workmen who are going to be affected by it should be beginning to take an interest in it. The scheme is unlike anything which is in existence in any other countries. Many of the continental countries have Unemployment Insurance schemes in operation, but they are mostly very small in extent and voluntary ,in character. This proposal now before our Parliament is the first effort to establish a scheme of Unemployment Insurance which is both State, National, and Compulsory.

===== 0002601_19111111_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
TO WHOM IT APPLIES.

Under this Bill every workman, skilled and unskilled, trade unionist or non-unionist, who is employed in certain scheduled trades will be compulsorily insured against unem- ployment. The trades which are scheduled in the Bill are Buildings, Construction of Works, Shipbuilding, Engineering, and Con- struction of Vehicles. The compulsory part of the Bill is limited at the outset to the

trades now mentioned, but it is provided that with the consent of the Board of Trade and the Treasury, other trades may be admitted to the scheme on the condition that

their inclusion will not involve the State in a contributbn exceeding a certain amount. THE RESPECTIVE CONTRIBUTIONS. The definition of a " workman 'S is such as to exclude clerks and firemen and persons under 18 years of age. Each workman who comes within the scope of the compulsory clauses of this Bill will have to subscribe 21/2d. a week to this Insurance Fund. This will be deducted from his wages by his employer, who will have to pay 21/2d. a week also for each workman he employs. There is no variation in the amount of the contri- bution. Whatever a workman's wages may be he will have to pay the same sum as the rest, and he will receive the same amount of out-of-pay. That is to say, that the highly I skilled mechanic and the unskilled labourer will each pay the same sum of 21/2d. a week to the Unemployment Fund, and the employer will add 21/2d. for each, and each will be able to claim the same weekly 'benefits. THE STATE'S CONTRIBUTION.

It may be added that *id. will have to be paid every week whether the workman makes a full week or not. The State con-

tribution to the Unemployment Fund is one- •••-• • • - • •

third of the total con-tril;utions of the work- men and employers—that is to say that the State will add one-third of sd. for each

insured pertion every week, which is a little over one-half of the contribution to be taken

from the workman. The State is responsible

in the first instance for the expenses of administration, but 10 per cent, of the income of the fund is to be paid over for the adminis-

tration of the scheme. This seems to be a very excessive sum to take for expenses, when it is remembered that the machinery

for the administration is already in iwistence, namely, the Labour Exchanges and the Trade Unions. The workman is not required to pay any contribution when he is.out of work : nor, so far as one can gather, will he be required to pay the arrears. Being relieved from payment when unemployed, and having the contribution stopped weekly when in work, there will be no possibility of arrears accumulating. THE WEEKLY.BENEFIT.

The _benefit which is to be paid .to an

insured workman who is out of work is to be 7s. a week. As the Bill stands at present, workmen engaged in the building trade are only given 6s. a week, though they pay the same contributions, but an indication has been given by the Government that this will be amended, and 7s. a week fixed for all the scheduled trades. The workman is not entitled to out of work pay unless he has 'been employed in an insured trade for 26 weeks. Benefit is not to be given if the unemployment is due to a strike or lock out. If a workman has lost his employment through misconduct, or has voluntarily left his employment without just cause, he shall not be entitled to out of work pay for six weeks from the date of losing or leaving employment. Other disqualifications are if he loses his work through imprisonment,or if he is an inmate of any public institution. Some of these disqualifications are lik ly . .

to operate with constderable hardship. W for instance, is to decide whether a man Las been discharged through misconduct, or w at misconduct is ? That provision will a . . _

great power into the hands of employeri. to deprive workmen of their unemployment pay where a workman, for some reason, may

• —. have made himself objectionable, as for instance by activity in Trade Unionism. Then again, a workman may leave his work voluntarily, but he may have been driven to do this by the intolerable conditions of his employment. These are points which will require very careful consideration by the Committee.

===== 0002601_19111111_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
LIMITED BENEFITS.

The workman is not entitled to remain on the fund for an indefinite period. No work- man is to draw more than 15 weeks of benefit in any twelve months, nor can he draw more than one week of benefit for every five weekly contributions paid. This restriction, it is said, is intended to prevent the chronic idler from sponging on the fund. The Board of Trade is given power to alter the scale of payments, and to reduce the period during which the benefit•may be paid. The administration of ,the Unemployment Fund is to be principally through the Labour Exchanges. When a man is out of work he will take his Insurance card to the Exchange, and an officer will examine his claim, and if it is alright in accordance with the regula- tions, the man will be entitled to draw 7s. a week after the expiration of the first week of unemployment. No benefit will be paid for the first week of any period of unemploy- ment. If a workman is dissatisfied with the decision of the Insurance Officer, he may

appeal to a Court of Referees, chosen from a panel representing an equal number of workmen and masters, with an independent chairman. It is expected that the Court of Referees will sit once a week in each district, so that no long delay need take place in settling claims. The workman will be put to no expense whatever by appealing. It may be noted that the workman will be required to sign the register at the Labour Exchange every day .when he is out of work.

===== 0002601_19111111_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
A NOVEL. PROPOSAL

There is one rather extraordinary and novel proposal in the Bill which provides that any workman who becomes repeatedly unemployed through lack of skill or know- ledge may be required to attend a suitable course of technical instruction, and if he fails to do so or to profit by such instruction, this fact may be taken into account by the Labour Exchange in deciding what is suitable employment for him. Trade Unions may form Associations of workmen in the insured trades, and these Associations may make arrangements with the Board of Trade to act in the place of the Labour Exchanges as the agency through which the benefit will be distributed. This trade union association will pay out the benefits and then make a claim for a refund 4rom the Board of Trade. There is also a provision which entitles the Trade Unions which pay unemployed benefit to receive from the States subsidy equal to one sixth of the amount they have paid. This will be of considerable benefit to•the trade unions, and should enable them to offer addi- tional benefits to those workmen above what will be paid to non-union workmen 'under the National scheme.

_ The Unemployment Insurance applies as we have said to only about half-a-dozen trades at once. No women are included in this part of the Bill. It will cover at the outset about 2,300,000 men. In this article I have merely stated the proposals of the Bill. On the whole this section of the Bill is more free from defects of detail than is the sickness part, but the Unemployment scheme is open to the fundamental objection that it puts the cost upon the wrong people.

1 CHILDREN'S CORWER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB. .3

DOGS THAT DO GOOD WORK.

Some years ago when I was in Bath I saw lat the station a little dog that collected for I a hospital. He had a neat little place to sit I on, a kind of large shelf and round his neck he carried a money box. When the station was quiet he enjoyed a nap and took no notice of anybody, but the moment a train came in Ihe sat up and began to bark and whine as lloud as he could. People looked round think- ing he was in distress, but this was only his little trick to get them to look at him. When they looked they saw his collecting box and of course felt obliged to give him a penny. I watched him many a time and studied his little tricks of trade. Since then I have read of many dogs that get a living. London Jack, at Waterloo Station, used to collect about £5OO per annum. His successor, London Jack 11., has learned many little winning ways that often bring him a shilling or two from many an unwilling passenger. When he comes into a compartment and finds a passenger absorbed in reading, he gently places one paw upon the knee of the passenger and looks up so pleadingly that it is almost impossible to resist the mute appeal. He can discriminate between the " flop" of the big flat penny-piece and the silver tinkle of the nimble sixpence. When you drop a penny into Jack's money-box, you get a somewhat indifferent bow of thanks. When, however, you put in a silver piece, Jack gives you an elaborate hand-shake with his paw. Many passengers who know the dog's tricks often give him money just to see the difference he makes between those who give pennies and those who give silver. Another immensely popular London dog collector is known to the world of passengers as Paddington Tim. His field of operation is Paddington Station, London's Great West End terminus. Tim is quite used to Royalty for it is at Paddington Station that the trains from Windsor arrive, and Tim is always on deck and very much in evidence whenever a Royal train pulls into the station. The ticket collectors always give Tim free access to Royal trains. He does not wait on ceremonial, but rushes up to the Royal coach and generally manages to pick out the King, Queen, or other eminent personages. It is the invariable custom of the Royal Family to contribute something to these begging togs at railway stations and Tim is never over- looked. In fact, he has come into very close contact with various members of the English ruling family. He is a particular favourite with little Princess Mary, who never fails to give him half-a-sovereign. Tim is also a great " friend " of Queen Alexandra; and the late King frequently noticed him.
