LOVE PRITCHARD, TtiE NEWLY-APPOINTED "KING" OF BARDSEY ISLAND HIS "CORONATION" WILL TAKE PLACE IN THE NEW YEAR.

and it was because of its isolation that its monarchy arose. Bardsey, with much of the adjoining mainland, belongs to Lord New- borough. The ancestor of the present Lord had a great affection for the romantic island and often spent time there. A generation or two ago when it was almost entirely , cut off from the world and when no one, in the winter, could be sure of being able to visit it at all,the inhabitants were scarcely amen- able to the laws of the country at all, and, without any disrespect or contempt for the laws of England, drew up a code of laws of their own. But rules need enforcement and, seeing the need of some directing influence, Lord Newborough selected a sensible peasant to be the ruler of the island. The man was chosen for his integrity and judgment and the owner vested in him his own authority as owner. LORD NEWBOROUGH " KINGMAKER. " With an eye for effect end perhaps not without a glimmer of humour he declared him King of Bardsey,and following the whim gave him a crown of brass, a treasure in the shape of a silver casket,and an army to guard it. The army consisted of a wooden soldier suitably painted. For many years King John Williams reigned and after him John Williams 11. reigned in his stead. KING LOVE THE FIRST. The next King will be Mr. Love Pritchard, who will probably go down to history as King Love I. The insignia of office will be brought forth, the wooden soldier will see the thing well and faithfully done, and King George V. will endure as best he can the indignity of a rival coronation and a rival monarch. It was in the palmy days of John Williams 11., that we first examined Bardsey, but things were not propitious and we did not get ashore. This summer we saw it again from the mainland, alluring and beautiful in its splendid isolation. THE ANCIENT HISTORY OF BARDSEY. From the water we can see the ruined tower of the Abbey of St. Mary a building founded, if tradition be correct, in the sth and 6th century. This flings us back to what perhaps ought to have been mentioned first, the ancient history of Bardsey. Strange to relate this lonely island was once one of the best known spots in Britain and it is confidently averred that the bones of twenty thousand saints lie buried there. The old chronicles are unanimous about it and it looks as though it must be true that in the days of the massacre of Christians under Ethelfrith the monks of Bangor Iscoed fled to Bardsey, establishing there an Abbey that gave fame to the island and made it a holy place. Within the Abbey walls many were laid to rest who had expressed a desire to be buried there and their bodies were carried by long slow stages, to the difficult ferry at Aberdaron. Then came the mania for pilgrimage. It was counted a holy place that must certainly confer blessing, and an unending string of pilgrims traversed the road—known to this day as the Pilgrim Road —through Carnarvonshire to the tip of the peninsular. MEMORIALS OF THE PILGRIMS.

A FINE HOLIDAY FIELD.

The Lleyn Peninsula is a charming though little known place for holidays. From Nevin or Pwllheli the visitor can cover a piece of unspoiled and most beautiful country. For mountaineergin The Rivals will satisfy any ' ordinary ambition,and antiquarian taste will revel in the City of the Giants and the numerous churches and Pilgrim wells. Abersoch and Aberdaron are charming watering places, "far from the madding. crowd" and dusty railway. So also are Morfa Nevin and Llanbedrog. At this last mentioned village there is, curiously enough, what is claimed to be the best Art collection in Wales. It is at the home of the late Love Jones Parry, now opened as a pleasure ground and art gallery. In the garden here we copied an inscription carved by some loving hand to the memory of a faithful dog. It is one of the many unexpected things that awaited us in this unexplored and altogether charming corner of Britain.

hardly be scratched without human bones coming to the surface. There is no doubt that it became a hugh cemetery in the early centuries and to the memory of these uncounted saints Lord Newborough has erected a beautiful memorial. He himself too was buried in the island he loved, and a fine Celtic Cross marks his grave. The population of the island, including the lighthouse staff, only numbers 64, though it may have jumped by one or two since the census. Farming and fishing are the only industries and the natives get their crabs, lobsters and produce across as best they can to Aberdaron.

THE LAND'S END OF WALES.

Last Saturday's newspapers blazoned forth the fact that Bardsey Island has chosen a new king, and gave pictures, more or less attractive, of this new member of the ruling classes. As captain of a fishing fleet no one would suggest an improvement. But as a real king! Of course there is a bit of history behind this Bardsey enthronement, and the announcement of the Coronation for New Year gives us the opportunity to spread it forth for our readers' entertainment.

Bardsey rises boldly from the sea at the tip of what is known as the Lleyn Peninsula, and is a shapely island of some two miles in length and a mile in width. It is the last scrappy bit of the storm bitten arm of land that pushes out into the Irish Sea,the "Land's End" of Wales,a neglected and most beautiful , bit of Britain. As we see it rising amid the sparkling waves it seems but a mere goat leap from the Carnarvonshire coast, but let the visitor beware. That small passage is one of the most treacherous upon our coast, and a trip can only be made when wind and tide combine. Many a day tripper has made the journey and been compelled to wait a week for a propitious tide to bear him home.

A -.LONELY SCRAP OF BRITAIN. This difficulty of access has left Bardsey rsland a painfully quiet and out-of-date place

CHAPTER VII.

LOTTA'S LOVE AFFAIRS.

Hunter had been quite right when he told Helen that the conditions of life at the Chase were changed. Sir Gregory was not like the same man. In • few weeks he aged ten years in appearance, and he had ceased to take any interest in the pursuits which had always given him plea- sure. He was dejected, nervous, and irrit- able, and spent the greater part of every day alone in his study, brooding over the wrong be had done Helen.

His conscience gave him no peace; and at the same time he lacked the moral courage necessary to avoi, his act and make redress. He had gone too far to retract, he told him- self. He could have pleaded at the time that the knowledge had taken him by sur- prise, and have given an explanation which would at least have saved him from the per- sonal humiliation which would be inevitable, after weeks of silence. The loss of all he had in the world ap- peared a small thing now compared with the shame of the confession he would have to make; and he felt that his one object must be to shield himeelf from that shame. Yet even as he made this decision he hated him- self for the cowardice which underlay it. And the consciousness of dishonour tortured him intolerably. The burden of the secret, too, crushed and saddened him, lic could not bear to be in the company of his wife, dearly as he loved her, for fear that some hint of it would escape him, and she would get to know him for the miserable, guilty coward he was in reality. - -•- _ .

The change in Sir Gregory affected every- one else in the household. Lady Trevellock was better again in health; but nervously anxious on account of her husband and his strange neglect of her. Mrs. Caulfield had been back to London, but had returned at her friends' earnest wish. She had carried out her promise to Sir Gregory in regard to Bul- mer, and it had been at her instance that the offer of the American appointment had come through Sir Gregory's bankers. "I suppose it is for the best that Bulmer should accept it, Beth?" said Lady Trend!. lock when discussing it with Mrs. Caulfield, who had not mentioned to her friend her part In the matter. "But I can't bear the thought of his going to America." "It appears to be a very good chance. He Is only to be away for a month or two, is he? And then he will settle down in the north." "I suppose I am a selfish old woman; but he has been so much with us, and I love to have him near me, that I can't bear the thought of separation. But one's children will grow up," she added with a little pathetic smile. "I had intended to buy the Charnock estates," said Mrs. Caulfield, meaningly. This estate was close to the Chase,. _ _

Lady Trevelloek sighed. "Ah, how, I wish It could have been, Beth! It would have been delightful to have had you both near us always." "We must make the best of things, dear," replied her friend. "And I am schooling my- self to accept the position." But she spoke very differently to Jasper Boatock on the subject. He had been com- ing and going between Lonaon and the Chase during the time, and was on the best of terms with the world and with himself, He had been shrewd enough not to force himself too much upon Sir Gregory; indeed he found the place too dull to care to be there for long: and as he had succeeded in obtaining a substantial loan from his victim, and had the prospect of getting more when he needed it, and had also found means of opening Mrs. Caul- &ld's long purse, he had been enjoying himself immensely. Ile had run down now to see her; and in the afternoon, when Sir Gregory was, as usual, alone in his study, and his wife watt asleep, they had a long and very earnest con- ference. .

"I have important news for you." he said; "but I'll tell you something A what I have done, and what I propose to do next. Luckily for us Miss Paxton ha a friend—a Mary Pea- cott—and I have managed to buy her." "To DUV her?" repeated (he widow. "Nct her ugly little body, of course; but all the information she can get. It was cheap at a hundred pounds." He had only paid twenty-five pounds; but lie was never too particular in money matters where his own interest intervened. "From her I got to know everything about the fair Helen; where she worked at typing; what firms sent her work; and at What housen she was accus- tomed to go for the piano-playing." "What was the use of that? '

"Patience, my dear madam, if you please. When I got wise about all that, I sized up the position and did some thinking. What I had to do was to cut off those sources of income. I schemed it all right, all right," he said, with a chuckle at his own cleverness. "I want to know the results, not the de- tails," said Mrs. Caulfield impatiently. "I guess I'll tell the thing in my own way. Well, I couldn't do anything direct, of course, because all sorts of questions might have been asked, and we should have run up against • snag or two. But I looked about for a num- ber of very shady people, with roguery and drink and worse written on their faces and their disreputable clothes, and for a con- sideration I induced them to go to ask for Miss Paxton at all these places—when she wasn't there, of course." he interjected with a wink. "And to make a little shine and drop awkward hints shout her. Result—no more work for Helen." "It was clever," was the other's comment ; P'but abominably cowardly." He laughed. "It was up to me to do something to earn your money, anyway, wasn't it? The Pescott girl, she's a peacl. for cunning, you bet, that girl; she started the idea. CHAPTER VIM PERSIOUTION AND A NZW DIAL

"Is Mrs. Chancellor at home'r'

"Yes, miss." "Ask her if she can conveniently see Ins for a minute ;" and Helen followed the neat maidservant into the morning-room and waited .while the message was taken. . . .

She had received a letter that morning from Mrs. Chancellor cancelling, without any reason, an engagement booked a few days before, and had resolved to try and find out the cause. It was the sixth eigagement cancelled in • the same fashion, and she could not believe that it was mere coincidence. When the servant returned there was a marked difference in her manner.

"Mistress is going out, and can't see you; and you are not to call again," the said curtly. Helen crimsoned at the tone. It confirmed her suspicions. "I wish yen would tell he that it is of great importance to me, ant that I am sure there is something which I can explain. I will not detain her a moment." _

"It wouldn't be any good; I was told not to leave you in the room alone," answered the girl. Helen fired at the insult; but an instant's reflection showed her the unwisdom of taking notice of it. "Very well," she said, and left the house. . . .

But she witi not going to submit without making an effort: and she wailed about close to the house until she saw Mrs. Chancellor come out, and then went up to her. "Please i excuse my speaking to you n this way. My name is Paxton, and a letter to me this morning—" "I have nothing to say to you, and I will not speak to you. Ido not want' you to- morrow night. That is all," was the un- compromising reply. "But I am convinced there is some m's- understanding. Please tell me what reason you have. It means so much to me," per- sisted Helen earnestly. "It is the sixth time it has happened to me, and—" "I am not compelled to give any reason, anti don't wish to be mixed up in anything unpleasant. I cancelled the engagement, as I had a perfect right to do; but if you insist upon being paid the half-guinea—" And she took out her purse. "It is not the money. That is the least part of it. But what you say shows me you must have some strong reason; and I beg you to tell it me." Mrs. Chancellor looked at her and hesi- tated; but then shook her head. "I will not have anything to do with it. If you want to know the reason, you can ask your friend, Dolly Letchmere. And now be good enough to leave me." . .

"But I don't know anyone of that name," protested Helen. "If you persist in annoying me, I shall call a policeman," declared Dirs. Chancellor angrily, as she quickened her pace and left Helen staring after her in blank amazement. After a moment's hesitation Helen retraced her steps to the house. "Hullo, you again. Mistress is out," said the maid rudely. "I came to ask you a question. Can you tell me anything about a woman, named Dolly Letchmere, who—" "No, I can't, and if I could I shouldn't. I know your sort;" and with an angry toss of the head she shut the door in llelen's face.

Helen returned home in great trouble. The thing was unaccountable. "Did you ever hear such a thing in your life, Mary?" she exclaimed, when she had told her friend.

"I don't believe it," was the reply. "That Chancellor woman's rich, and a rich woman would tell any lie to get out of paying people." "But she offered me the money in the street; and it was clear from her manner that there was something behind, a..<3 she didn't want to be mixed up in it. She said so in fact. But what on earth can it mean? I was never so puzzled in my life before." "There's a mistake somewhere, that's all. You're not going to ask me to believe that someone is taking the trouble to go about behind your back to set people against you? Who's going to do that, I should like to know? Besides, who's to know where you are engaged? It's ridiculous, Helen, to talk like that." __ - _

"But she gave me the name. Dolly Letch- mere, and called her my friend."

"She's probably a fool; most of those fat rich women are. Perhaps someone went to the house, and, besides being a fool, she. thought it had something to do with you, and got this bee in her bonnet. I shouldn't take an/more notice of it." "But it's the sixth time it has happened; and all my typing work has gone as well, or nearly all, and without any reason. It can't all be just chance." "There's ups and downs with us all; and mostly downs for people like us," was the characteristically gloomy reply. "I'll go round to every one of the others this afternoon and try to get to the bottom of it. I shan't take this lying down. I can't afford to, indeed." She spent the whole afternoon in making the inquiries; but returned home without having found out anything. In every case she had been refused an interview. Not con- tent with this, she wrote to each of the ladies, setting out the facts and asking why the en- gagements had been cancelled. But no result followed, and her spirits suffered in consequence. And the following evening Mary Petreott had a surprise for her. "I shouldn't let it worry me, Helen," she said when they were discussing the matter. "You're going to marry Mr. Trevellock, and you can afford to laugh at all this." "Would you laugh if you found your work slipping sway in this fashion?" "I should if I was going to be married; but speaking of work, I think I've got some- thing at last. It's in the country. Just as well, as you're going away." "You haven't told me anything about it, dear. I am glad." "I didn't say anything because you were worried, and this wasn't certain;" and she went on to describe what the position wee she had obtained. "I shan't have to go be- fore you're married, I think. You have made up your mind to marry, haven't you?" Helen sighed. "I don't know what to do," she said. "Oh, now, isn't that stupid of me," ex. claimed Mary. "This telegram came for you while you were out, and thinking it might be important or something I could do for you, I opened it." "It's all right," said Helen with a smile. It was from Bulmer to say that he was coming to town on the following day, and mast see Helen the first thing. "We know what it means," laughed her friend. "It's a good thing that I got as engagement in time, eh?" The thought of seeing her lover brought a look of happiness to Helen's face. "I am almost afraid of making the deci- sion, Mary," she said. "I shouldn't hesitate if I had the chance; but nobody wants a wizened-faced little beset like me." "I shall quarrel with you if you call your. self names like that, Mary. You're the only friend I have in London," and she bent across and kissed her. "Shall you worry Mr. Trevellock with all this Mrs. Chancellor business and the rest of it. Helen?" asked Mary after a pause. "I don't know. I shall see. I'm very full of it just now ; but, as you say, it might worry him; and if I--if Ido deride as he wishes, it tv.,,uldn't do ant good.'l

bet' coutdu t du anything either, euidti

"No. It seems that no one can do any. thing." Satisfied with this answer, Mary Peseott said no more about it. . .

When Bnlmer came in the morning, Helen saw at once that he was worried; and the cause soon came out.

"I have some news, and for the life of me I don't know whether you'll be glad or sorry to hear it; but I know beastly well that I'm sorry." "Tell " "I'm afraid you're going to get your own way after all, unless we make our minds to take a double risk." . . . . .

"That isn't telling me much," she laughed. "It's about our marriage, dearie. I had letter from that fellow Burrell, and he says that he forgot to tell Inc that one of the con- ditions of the appointment was that the man getting it must he unmarried. lie says that the place isn't fit for a decent Englishwoman to go to, and that he prefers to have a man with no ties of any sort. I think it's a fool of a reason, and the man must be an ass. I haven't answered him yet. I oame up to see you first, of course. I'm ready enough to take the risk of marrying all the same, if you are; or I'll chuck the whole thing and look for something else." "You mustn't do that, Bulmer, mustn't do either for my sake. I think I am rather re• lieved to hear it, I was no puzzled what to "Hadn't you made up your mind then?" ho asked with a quick glance. "Don't ask inconvenient questions, sir," she laughed. "I'm quite content to wait. And just think what a delightful prospect there will be to look forward to. You'll go out there and do well, and rush things into order for this Mr. Burrell; and then you'll come home and ----" _ . . .

He caught her round the waist and drew 11.. q, to him. "I shall be working for the dearest little woman in the world, shb'll be waiting here - —" "Counting the days till you return," she put in, "and living in suet': hope---" At that point the talk became somewhat in articulate, with intervals such as only lovers understlnd. . . .

When it was over at last, and Helen had laughingly smoothed her hair, Bulmer said : "Are, you busy to-day, or can you come on th, river?" _ . .

"I have nothing at all to do to-day," she declared.

-"Tii-e; we'll do it together," be cried gladly. "Get your hat and a wrap and we'll be off." . .

As she was getting ready Ilelen eon. siderA whether she should tell him her

troulLes; and a remark he made as they were leaving the house, decided her not to do so. "I didn't expect such luck as this. You're generally no full up with work. But it's a b using, too. If I didn't know that you were doing well here, and would be able to wait for my return in comparative comfort, wild horses wouldn't make me leave you behind, Helen." . _ .

"I have not been quite so busy lately, but I soon shall be again," she answered with a smile. And she ass glad that she had not spoken. Nothing must be allowed to inter- fere with his accepting the chance which had offered. Their whole future might depend upon that. The thought held her silent for a while, and he rallied her, asking jokingly what her thoughts were. "Of the lovely day we are going to have together, Bulmer. Perhaps the last for ever so long," she said ruefully. "But I shan't let the time seem long." "You face the parting more cheerfully than I can," he replied, with a shake of his bead.

"I shall fix my thoughts on your return, _

They talked first of his journey; and he told her all he knew of the work he would have to do; and late in the afternoon, when they were again speaking of America, she began to tell him of her own life in the States; her childish recollections of her mother; her years with her relatives in Mas- sachusetts; and then of what she had heard from her mother's relations about her father;

... - "I am afraid he must have been a very bad man, Bulmer—bad, even for the west of America at that period. His desertion of us was cruel enough, but I'm afraid that wasn't by any means the worst thing he ever did." "It doesn't trouble me, Helen; and I shouldn't let it worry you." But it seems almost uncanny to have to think of one's father as bad. You'd feel as I do if you could imagine Sir Gregory any. thing but the soul of honour. Ata my Aunt Deborah used to say that Powell of the Black Gulch, as he was called, was one of the worst men in Montana. But, Of course, she was prejudiced, for my dear mother's sake. And mother was one of the whitest women that ever lived." "What name did .you say! Powell?"

"I declare I forgot I had never told you that my real name is Powell. I told Sir Gregory when I was at the Chase, and I asked him to tell you. I suppose he forgot it, and I never thought to ask you." and she explain on to expla how she had taken her mother's name.

"My father, certainly, never told me; but he was right that it didn't matter. It isn't your name I care about, sweetheart, it's yourself. Did you ever change it legally?" "Not that I know of. My mother took her family's name to prevent her ever being found by my father. But there was never much fear of that, I should think. Massa- chusetts is too far from Montana to have made that likely." "By jove, I say, there's a singular thing. mMy father u i insist have been out n Montana about that time. He was there as a young man, you know; years before he came into the Chase, of course; and his name then was Powell. I wonder if he ever heard anything of your father? I'll ask him when get home. Are you cold, child?" he broke off suddenly, seeing her start and shiver. "No. It's nothing," she answered in some confusion. "Yet I did shiver, didu't I? I—l am always troubled when I think of my father."

Then we won't think of him any more.

I'll pull over into the sunlight. You are looking quite pale. I'm sure you are cold. And you're looking as cad as if we had to say good-bye now. "I'm all right again now," she replied, with a smile. "Hullo, look at those idiots in that boat ever there. They'll be in the river in a minute; fooling about in that rotten fashion." In this way his attention was distracted from Helen, and the talk turned afterwards into other channels. She was very thoughtful for the rest of the day. She told Bulmer it was because they were to part so soon, and he tried to cheer her up. But the remembrance of that scene with Sir Gregory at the Chase was strong in her thoughts all the evening, and a horrible fear which she dared not put in definite shape even to herself had been roused by the after- noon's conversation. "Can there have been two Gregory Powells in Montana at the same time, both English? If not—" the unspoken alterna- tive kept her awake all the long night., (To be continued.)

The lady, however, comes with a determination to refuse the money and in this she is successful. CITIZEN SUNDAY AT LYMM.

What has of late years come tote known as "Citizen Sunday" was observed on Sunday, when the annual service promoted by the executive of the Lymm Trades- men's Association was held at the Parish Church. Reprexentatires of all claws and denominations were present. The procession, which started from the Cross, was headed by the Lymm Subscription Band and the police under the direction of Sergeant Washington. The route taken was Eagle-brow, Booths-hill and Brookfield. Among those present at the church were Colonel Legb. the Honourable John E Cross, Mr Alfred Watkin, and Mr W Peter Ryland, (county magistrates ; members of the Lymm Urban Council, Messrs 0 0 Smith (chairman), Thomas Warburton (vice-chairman), W G Groves, S Davies, R H Gotobed, Frederick Lockyer, J Hardy Holt, Dr E DI de Jong (medical officer), D Mort (surveyor), W L Donaldson, (gas manager) ; members of the Tradesmen's Associa- tion, Messrs F E Evans (president), D Daniels (bon secretary), W S Hodgson (hors treasurer), WE Hillier, A B Richardson J Hatton, W Besgick, J Rogerson, H S Brown and J W Gibbon ; members of the Manchester Unity of Oddfellowe, Messrs P Lester (grand master), Herbert Whitelegg (deputy grand master and secretary of Lymm Lodge), F Webster (Ca), John Whitlow (tressumr), R Brooke, F Holt, it Chorlton, Proffit, W Holt (secretary Foresters) and F Druce ; the Eleatley Boy Scouts under the direction of Messrs T Nash, Webb, Jun. and Kay ; the High Legh Boy Scouts, under the care of Messrs C W Surridge Harold Melrose; the members of the Lycum Fire Brigade. The service was opened with the processional hymn, "Onward, Christian Soldiers," and the National Anthem The service was taken by the Rev Ci B Thurston, rector of Lymm, and the Rev Horace Gray, headmaster of the Warrington Grammar School, and the lesson was read by the Rev E P Wethered, rector. of St. Peter's, Ought, ingtcn. The special preacher was the Rev J H

r Thorpe, vicar of St. George's, Stockport, a hose tent was" Thou shalt worship the Lord thy God." He said be was sorry to say that there was spreading throughout England an indifference to religion and attendance at the House of God. It was not only in the Church of England, but in every religious body. What were the causes which lead men to neglect God? Some men abandoned God for the making of money. Their creed was " mate money honestly if possible, but make it." Sunday labour was another thing which prevented men from attending a place of worship. He earnestly implored all employers of labour present to do without all unnecessary Sunday labour and see that the day of God's rest was observed. Another reason for the falling off of religious worship was the mistakes made by religious reople themselves. So much bad been made of preaching that it inverted the true order of things. A sermon was not a kind of entertainment Preaching was of no use unless it touched the conscience end made people serve God better. It was a very serious thing that one of the reasons why men did not attend church was that they had persuaded themselves thst religion and morality were not one. Men might have a very fine code of morality and yet not be Christians. The morality of many Mohammedans put some so called Christians to shame, judged by the kind of lives they lived. Real religion gave men health and strength to resist those things wkich were wicked. One of the great reasons for public worship was the law of association. Voltaire, a very clever man, said if only they could destroy Sunday they could soon destroy the Christian religion. The gathering together of men and women in the house of God was one of the things which upheld religion. They should not forget Christ's promise that " where two or three are gathered together in His name He would grant their requests." The assemblies in the house of God were but rehearsals for the future life.

Stockton Heath. PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.

The monthly meeting of the Parochial Committee was held at the Hall Cafe on Thursday (writing when the following members were present :—Meat J H Clarke (chairmen), J Whitly, F Pearson, W Mclntyre, I, Rose, J Hallows, C Hallows, J McDermid, CI F Ashton (clerk), rut J Swinton (surveyor).

THE B;n3TION or ELECT6RETT. THE DEMAND TO DECIDE.

A lengthy discussion took place on the question of the proposed substitution of the gas lighting of the village by electricity. It was eventually decided, on the motion of Mr. McDermid, that s circular should he sent to every house in the parish, stating that as the Council had unani- mously recognised the advantages of a public supply of electricity in the district, they bad approached the Mesey Power Co., Ltd, who had Parliamentary powers authorising them to eupply a current in the Rural District of Runcorn under the Electric Lighting Acts, with a view to asking them to Extend their mbins in that neighbourhood at an early date. The extension was at present under considered,- n and the mains would be laid if sufficient demand for power and lighting was received. The circular sets fort: the proposed charges for the electricity, power and lighting. It states that at the rate of 4d. a sixteen candle power lamp to bum for fifty hours could be qbtained, while a twenty-fire candle power lamp for thirty hours and a thirty-two

candle cower lamp for twenty-ere boom, could be obtained at a aimilar rate.

_ Mr. Rose slid that the circular did not draw any comparison betvre(n the cost of gas nod electricity. Mr. McDermid : I don't think it is advisable to draw comparisons. . .

Mr. Hallows also held t his view, stating that they did not want to cause any friction in Warrington.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

DOMESTIC TRAGEDY.

An inquest was held by Coroner S. Brighouse, at Widnes, on Saturday, on Elizabeth Wailing,aged thirty- flee, wife of Richard Wailing, of 134, Milton-road, Widnea. The hnsband said a letter (produced) was in his wife's handwriting. She had been in poor health. At Christmas lest she had • stroke of paralysis, but eight weeks ago she seemed to improve. On Tuesday last she ~out out and was not again seen alive. On Thursday her body was recovered from the Widnes and St. Helens Canal. In a note which was read to the jury, the deceased praised her husband and mother, but complained of Spain in her bead. The coroner and jury expresaed sympathy with the husband and mother, and the jury returned a verdict of " Suicide whilst of unsound mind."

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. At the usual meeting on Tuesday, Mr. J. S. Spruce presiding, the clerk reported having received statistics of poor law relief throughout England and Wales. The percentage throughout the country was 26 per 1,000 and in the Runcorn district 157 per I,OOQ, which was highly satisfactory. In reply to Mr. Linaker, jun., the Clerk said it was impossible to compare this year with past years because of the effect of old age pensions.

AIATTES,

It was decided that the usted Christmas fare for the Inmates and officers be provided. Thanks were tendered to Mrs. Howird of Orchard Croft, He!shy. for gifts of toys and flowers; and to Mrs. Selby of Frodsbam, for gifts of provisions. The resignation of Nurse Cooke was reported and it was decided to advertise for applicants. It was reported that the number of inmates was 204, the largest number for many years ; 74 vagrants had been relieved during the pest year. RUNCORN RURAL COUNCIL.

At Dutton on Tuesday, Mr. T. Wright presiding. The Local Government Board wrote with reference to Dr Adams, the medical officer, having omitted to forward them statistics of cases of infectious diseases in the CoucciTs district in time for inclusion in their weekly return. In the six weeks ending October 21st be Ind omitted three times to provide the returns and they as‘md the Council to obtain a written explanation from him and to forward it with their observation. . .

Dr. Adams produced his written reply which expressed his great regret for any inconvenience caused and his aesortince that it would not occur again. He explained that he had been taking his holidays in week- ends during the year and that bad led him to overlook the matter. He bad now arranged a method which would work automatically. He RIO not the rnly culprit by any means as the return showed innumerable omissions.

LAND ENCLOSURE,

The County -Council reported that they proposed appointing a special sub-committee to consider the matter for the encloswe of some land at the junction of the Keckwick and Preston Rrook-road with the Moore main road by Sir Richard Brooke's agents. As the Council were interested in one of the roads they suggested they should send representatives. The Clerk said the Serves or had attended the meet- ing which had bees held on Monday, end the Council had undertaken to forward him plans showing exactly what it was proposed to do.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS AND C.M.S. £760 CONTRIBUTED.

The yearly public meeting of the St. Helens auxiliary of the Church Missionary Society was held on Monday night at the Town Hall. The Rev. Albert Baines, vicar of St. Helens, presided, being supported by a large number of local clergy and several missionaries. The yearly atatement was prerented by Mr. Hugh Gamble (hon. treasurer). The auxiliary, he said, had sent ep considerably more than the previous year, but that was not owing to the amount collected by the different parishes, but to the sum produced by the children's sale of work. The amount raised by the parish church and its missions was .£4t3, against £523 last year. They had a special collection for the building fund of Mango Cathedral, Uganda, which raised £lO. The young people's missionary sale or work produced .6152, and they cent up a total of £761, against £651 last year. That was an increase of about £7O ; but, said Mr. Gamble, if the work was to be kept up, they must have more subscriptions, for the society last year had a deficit of £36,000, notwithstanding the strictest economy.

FOOTBALL AND RELIGION.

AUSTRALIANS AT A ST. HELENS SERVICE. A large attendance of football officials and supporters assembled on Sunday afternoon at the weekly meeting of the Wesleyan Men's Brotherhood, St. Helens, when Mr. Arthur Bridgett, of the Sunderland team gave an address. Dr. Wilson presided at the ottset, owing to the late a.rrival of Mr. C. H. Ford, manager of the Australian Rugby team, whose journey from Southport was delayed by a motor mishap. Among those also present were Messrs Wilfrid Ellison (St Helens Rocs), A Rudd, J F May, and H S Turtill (St Helens Rugby), II L Kimmins, the Rev Barron Renton and later Messrs , Ford, J H Houghton (secrstary of the Northern Rugby League), and members of the Australian team. Mr. I Travis sang two solos. Dr. Wilson said acme people unfortunately did not care for football, but that was simply because they did not mix with football people or go to see it played.' If more members of the various churches joined in the 1 pursuit, and patronised the matches, they would be bound to exert an influence which could only be for the betterment of a game. Mr. Bridgett gave an interesting address, advising footballers and everyone else to play the game on the only sound basis of honesty, fairness and religion. Mr. C. H. Ford, who arrived late, expressed his admiration for the gathering. In Australia, he said, Sunday was the day on which the people went in for pleasure. That was the first footballers' service he had ever heard of, and, though the colonials regarded themselves as more go ahead than the mother country, he thought they would do well to copy England in that. In Australia they had a Church Union, in con- nection with which all sorts of competitions were run all the year round, and they had in that way got one of the strongest competitions of any he had heard. Perhaps in that respect the mother country might copy them. (Applause). When he got back to Australia, he would be able to tell his friends of what they had done that afternoon, and see if they could do something to I follow their example.

" MILLION MORE MEMBERS."

TEMPERANCE MISSION AT Sr. HELENS.

A mission connected with the St.Helens and District Band of Hope and Temperance Union was opened on, Saturday,in the Wind's Pilkington Hall,at the V.M.C.os. The president (Mr. J. B. Liach, J.P.) and Mrs Leach held a reception in the afternoon,including Mr Chandos Wilson. secretary, and Mr. By. Bloom, spent of the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Unron. Mr. Leach afterwards presided at a public meeting when he stated that the mission was held with the object of furthering the " Million More Members " movement, in which all Banes of Hope. Sunday schools scd Christian sesociations should assist. It bad come to be recognised .that erre of the most vital and important questions in our national life was the upbringing and protection of the children. The people all realised that every child was an asset to the nation and must be regarded end treated as such. But in addition to its commercial value, the child had en immortkl scut, and their efferts were to save the child for the best purposes of the nation's life, and to see to it that at the end it should leave the world better for its life and work. The King and Queen were patrons of that mission, and swished them every success in the work.ln St. Helens they had a splendid army of Band of Hope workers, and he was sure they would take their full share in promoting the success of the work. Addresses were also given by Messrs Wilson and Elinor, and the Rave. W. H. Jeffries, B. Fell, and W. Holroyd.

LORD NEWT ON AND CANADIAN SENTIMENTS Speaking at a Conservative conference in Manchester on Satcrday, Lord Newton said he had only just returned from Canada, and was there when the election took place. One reason for the result was the undoubted fear on the part of the Canadians of being eventually annexed by the United States. Another reason was that there was a keen desire on the part of the vast majority of Canadians to maintain the Imperial connection. That sentiment really had a great deal to ,do with the rt suit of the election. A third reason was that the Canadians were determined to have nothing whatever to do with anything epprosching Free Trade. They had observed too closely some of the results of Free Trade in this country. The Canadian election constituted about the biggest sell " which had ever taken place in the biltory of international politics. In the first place it was a gigantic " sell," for the American politicians, who thought they were exploiting the circumstances for their own advantage ; and in the second place it was an even greater " sell " for the British Covernment, because that Government, what- ever they might say, took part in that election and did their best, perhaps not openly but behind the scenes, to further the Reciprocity agitation.

COLLECTING FOR HER SALARY. At the Borough Police Court, on Tuesday, Ruth Millar, of 7, Bartlett-street, Wavertree, Liverpiol, wan remanded on bail in connection with a charge of collect- ing charitable contributiots by false pretences. Mr. Forshaw, inn., defended. The Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls) said the woman came to Warrington on Monday from Liverpool, Her first call was on a number of railway clerks at the , Cheshire Lines Station from whom she obtained small sums of money on the representation that eh*, was a collector for the Home for Destitute Children at Tuebrook. She afterwards called at the office of the Borough Treasurer and imp, i red for Mr. Woodcock. She was interviewed by Mr. G. Timperley, who, after hearing her statement, wilt for Mr. Woodcock. On the return of Mr. Woodcock the woman had decamped. The police station was her next visit, where she interviewed him (the chief-constable). Sh• produced a elm which showed that Inspecter Philipps had given here sub- scription in 1907, and ahe asked for a renewal. He questioned her stout the institution and the donations, whereupon she became very abusive, sod sulnequently admitted that she was not a collector for the home, but was collecting for her salary. He warned her if she con- tinued the practice in Warrington she would hear more about it. On leaving the police station she was watched by Sergeant Stuart, who saw her make several calls upon local shopkeepers. She was afterwards appre- hended. Defendant admitted that for several }ears she had been a collector for the home in question, but owing to her health giving way she was collecting for her salary with the permission of the authorities of the home. _

A remand for seven days was granted, bail being allowed in one surety of £2O. EX-MAYOR AND THE BENCH. -

Before the commencement of the usual bosiness at the Borough Court on Monday, the Mayor stated that that was the last occasion on which Alderman Forshaw would sit as a magistrate, having occupied that posit ion during the past year by virtue of his office as the ex-Mayor. He expressed his regret that such a time had cone but be thanked Alderman Forshsw for the assistance that he had always rendered during his (the Mayor's) term of office. Mr. A. Browne, on behalf of the solicitors, alto expressed regret at Alderman Forshaw's severance from the Bench but at the same time hoped he would soon be enabled to resume magisterial duties by his appoint- men.t.as a justice.. . . . .

Alderman Forshaw said that be had always tried to the best of his ability to execute j entice and maintain truth, while he had occupied that position, and he was only too pleased to be of any assistance.

Competitors must have two Saturday roan before close of entries on Saturday November 23th, the scrutiny meeting will be held on Monday, November 27th. CONTRACTOR.

Councillor Lashford and his Opponent. Sir,— Having noticed a report in two local papers with regard to an utterance saki to have been made by Mr. James Hampson, the Socialist candidate at the recent municipal elections, I should be glad of an opportunity—through your columns—to submit a little evidence. Mr. Lashford, speaking at the Conservative Club, said his opponent had called him a "rogue" s hen ccming down the Town Hall steps. I accompanied Mr. Hampson from inside the Town Hall to Sankey-street. Mr. Lash- ford was nowhere near, never even irr sight to my knowledge. To say my comrade made such an utterance on the steps is an absolute lie. I heartily endorse Mr. Hampson's "bitter out-burst" for I believe it was truth ; at the same time I think it only fair that anything attributed to him as the above should receive denial. I should have thought sufficient falsehood was circulated before the election to have satisfied aryone for at least twelve months,— losses truly,

83, Lythgoes-lane, Warringioo. Bth Novtrober, 1511

ISAAC SHAW,

Honouring a Schoolmaster. Sir, —Through the medium of your valuable columns, let me add my testimony of praise to my old schoolmaster, Mr. John Wright of Daresbury. Through circumstances over which I had no control, (could not be present at the 25th anniversary of Mr. Wright's headmastership of the Daresbury Schools. I should like to give a column of my recollections of Daresbury School and Mr. Wright, but I suppose space will not allow ? I cannot (as rue of Mr. Wright's old scholars) speak too highly cf the man. As a mastt r he was a strict disiplinsrian but underneath his stern discipline there was bidden a kind heart. A fine personality, it was a far keener sting for a scholar to be spoken reprovingly to by his master than even the sting of his cane. (Many of the latter mysterously found their way to the stove.) Only an old scholar dare remind him of these daring deeds, but this is by the way. Personally, his influence has counted for much in my own career. I am often confronted with advice long given by my old master which has always stood me in good stead. It is not my opinion only. but that of many of his old scholars when I say that Mr. Wright is almost revered by them. The same can be said of his good wife, who was always ready to help when needed. Many a scholar has met with practical sympathy at her hands in cases of sickness. My wanneet congratulations go out to Mr. Wright and also to Mrs. Wright, whose influence in Daresbury schools counted for much.— Yours obediently,

C rappenhall.

GEORGE A. LEACH.

What about the Band?

- - Sir,—Are we to understand by the silence of Mr. B. S. Townroe that be is satisfied with his letter to the "Daily Dispatch" (vhich was reprinted in your valuable men), and that he does not think it worth while to give credit where credit is due? At least, one expected some explanation by him of his seeming neglect of duty. Such "tactics" in time of war may be excused, for we are reminded of the old tag that "all's fair in love and var." Further, we are told by a writer, in last month's number of "The nation in Aims," when describing an attack on "a mythical enemy " :—" The blood coursed through cue quickly then, and the civilizing influences of all the centuries seemed instantly to be dropped as the primeval instinct of ell-preservation asserted itself." But we live in a time of peace and there appears to be no reason why one should be unfair to anyone— even to advance one's own" cause.' Again, in a report of the Parr Hall meeting, in this month's "The "'Cation in Arms." there is not one single word of public thanks to the Band. In fact, the band is ignored absolutely, and credit alone is given to the objects cf the League. Now, sir, I would ask Mr. Townroe to be good enough to answer the following question :—" Does he believe that the Parr Hall would have been racked at 7.15 p.m. for the meeting of the National Service League to commence at 8 p.m., if Crosfields' Band had not been advertised to play? "—Thanking you. yours truly,

EDMUND OSBORNE,

22,1d-atreet. Warrington. November 10th, 1910.

"OUR NEW FREEMAN."

HONOURICONFERRED UPON MR ROBERT PIERPOINT

A Chorus of Eulogy—PoLtii

The Freedom of the Borough was conferred noon , Mr. Robert Pierpoint, who for fourteen years repre- ented Watrington in Parliamen., at a special meeting of the Town Council held at the Town Hall on Thurs- day immediately after the election of Mayor. Great interest was taken in the pleasing ceremony, and in addition to a full attendance of members of the Council, there was a large gathering of the public. Prominent townspeople present included Sir Gilbert Greenell, Bart, C.V.O . and Mr. W. Peter Rylands. Mr. Pierpoint, who was accompanied by his wife, was greeted with lend applause on tat irg his seat on the right of the Mayor. The scion bearing the resolution conferring the Freedom upon Mr. Pierpoint uas formally presented by the Mayor. The silver casket, in which the scroll will be encicsed,had not been completed in time for the ceremony, but the casket presented to the late Alder- man James Fairclough on a similar occasion ens brought into requisition. The scroll is illum'nated upon vellum, and is mounted upon silk with ivory roller and silk fringe at foot. The Warrington Arms appear fully coloured , at the head of the address, the mantling running I 'down and forming the border, variety being gained by the introduction of finer detail. A striking feature of the design is the r:chly illuminae miniature showing symbolic figures of Warr;ngton , and " Fame." Warring ou in depicted sea typical . ironworker surrounded by the feature connected , with the town's industry—roll of wire, chains, and , other iron manufactures. This figure is presenting to • '' Fame" a scroll upon which is seen Mr. I'ierpoint's I name. " Fame "is shown as a female figure richly , did, and is crowned with a wreath of laurel. Thrcugh , a window in the miniature is teen a typical view c.f a

blazing furnace. The lettering is plain. distinct end refined and the address is signed by the Mayor and Town Clerk and has the Corporation Seal affixed at the foot.

The wording of the Scroll is as follows: COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. GEORGE WM. JOSEPH, M.D. MAYOR. At a special Meeting of the Council of the County Bcrough of Warrington convened pursuant to the provisions of the Honorary Freedom of Boroughs Act 1885, and held in the Town Hall on Thursday the Pth day of November 1911, it was unanimously resolved that the Honorary Freedom of the Borough be and the same is hereby conferred upon Robert Pierpoint Esq , J.P., of St. Austins, Warrington, and he is hereby admitted to be an Honorary Freeman of tbe Borough in rec-gnition of the eminent services which he has rendered to the Borough. GIVEN under the Common Seal this 9th day of November, 1011. •

Mr. ROBERT PIERPOINT, who had a great reception on rising to return thanks, said :—I have had to make many speeches in my time and some of them have been Vf ry difficult indeed, but I apprehend that ' the speech I now have to make is perbape the most difficult I over had to approach in my life. To sit here, as I have done for a considerable time, hearing praises which perhars ought to have been said when I was not present, has been in one way painful, but in another way very, very gratifying. (Hear, hear.) Sir, it cantirt but be a very gratifying thing to a soon to receive the thanks and the kind appreciation of a community in which he is well known, but when that c mmunity is the town in which he was born and Of which his father was the third Mayor, then the gratification is enhanced and the kindnss appears very much greater. I thank you, Mr. Mayor, for having proposed in such courteous and kind Language that this Freedom should be given to me. I have to thank Alderman Tinnion for having seconded that propoeition and all the aldermen and courcillors who have supported it. I thank you all and I may say this for myself—that in the speechee,

perhaps too flattering—(" No, no ")—I hope not much too flattering, which hay, been made to-day, I have seen the consummation of what I made one of my objects while I represented this town in the House of Commons. From the moment that I was returned as Conservative member for Warrington, I made up my mind that courtesy should go as much as possible between party and party in this town—(hear, hear) and that it should make no difference in personal intercourse whether a man belonged to my side or to those who were opposed to me. To-day I have seen the consummation of that, because I have heard speeches made by those who have, as Mr. Roberts very properly said, justifiably opposed me in the past, and I have seen that, at all events, I did something to encourage that friendly feeling in the town which I hope will long continue. (Applause.) It would be difficult for me to thank you sufficiently for what has been done. You have given me a very high honour indeed to-day and I think you may trust me that I shall never disgrace it. (Applause,) You have alluded to the fact that my wife and I are leaving, or have left Warrington. It has been a very great wrench to us to do so. but you have given on some comfort, for when we look at the beautiful casket and read, as we shall read over and over again, this beautifully engrossed address, it will help us, though we shall want no help at all, to remember the old place. (Applause.) I cannot but think -ifl am wrong, please let me nurse my little conceit—that the honour which you have done me to-day is a sort of outgrowth of the feeling that some of the older ones amongst you have for the memory of I my father. I take it that had there been the Freedom of the Borough in his time, he would have been on that honoured list. It did not exist in those days, but I may take it as my own belief which I hug to my heart, that what I have said is true. I take it as a high honour that I should have been allowed to have this given to me. I thank you all for the kindness with which you have spoken of me. I thank you especially for the unanimity with which the resolution has been passed. I thank you for the honour you have done me and, may I add that, standing as I do by the side of the Mayor, the honour I feel it that the Mayoress has graced this room with her presence to-day. I am sure you will excuse me if I speak no na,,re now. It has been a difficult speech to make. I thank you from my heat t. (Loud applause.)

Warrington Socialist Soddy,

In connection with the above society a lecture waa given on Sunday evening at the Socialist Hall. ilderspool. the attendance being exceptionally good and promising well for the aeries of weekly lectures arranged for the winter. Mr. A. Spencer was chair- man, and Mr. Will Hughes, of Manchester, speaker. Subject :—Poverty," Disease and Death—Mr. Hughes gave some startling figures obtained from two districts in Manchester ; one being populated by the working class, the other, the so-called middle class. The striking difference in the number of sufferers and victims, particularly in infantile mortality, showed clearly the great sacrifice of humanity in the densely populated industrial centres. The speaker remarked that the huddling together of the producers of the nation's wealth, in such a way as to make possible such terrible results, was a disgrace to any civilized community. Good housing, fresh air, clothing and pure food were absolutely necessary to every individual to enable them to combat disease,especially consumption. These essentials the workers were denied because of the miserable pittance received in retina for their wearying uil. Mr. Lloyd-Gearge to remedy this, proposed ti tax the worker sevenpenee in the pound. This by some marvellous twist, not yet defined, would bring relief and jiy to all. Such schemes as Mr. Lloyd-George hal recently prop:sell, would make the poverty of the workers more acute, and he, the spesker, failed to see how any intelligent person cou!d believe otherwise.

A Local Charity.

About eighty of the aged paor participated in the annual distribution of blankets and other comfort, in connection with Tbewlis's Charity, which took place at the office of the Charity Tovestigation Sviety. Bank-street, on Saturday morning. The annual income of the charity amounts to Sol. Among those present at the distribution were the Mayor and Mayorees. Mr. E. A. White and Mrs Harrison.—The Mayor, in an address to the recipients, said it was a very excellent charity, and they ought all to feel very grateful to the late Mr. Thewlis for his generosity. They should also feel grateful to the ladies, and particularly Mrs. Harrison, vrtio had spent theincome so carefully and to the best advantage. Lady Greenall and other ladies contributed money in order to help those who required help. (Applause) —Mr. E A. White proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayor and Mayoras, for attending. and the proposition was carried amidst applause. A few words as to the origin of the charity may be of interest. Mr. Shaw Thewlis. who was the proprietor of the file and tool works known as Thewlis and Griffith, in ifersey-street. by his will directed that .£5Oll should, after the death of his wife, be invested and known as "Thewlis'e Chants" and that the income thereof should annually on tl).;e lit day of November, which was his birthday, be applied in supplying blankets and other comforts to tbe aged poor of the borough of Warrington, without regard to their religious belief. Shortly after the death of Mrs. Thewlis. which took place on the 4th day of February, Itt92, r_.-0q was paid by the trainees appointed by the will to the official trustee of charitable funds. and that sam was invested by the official trustee in the r,nrchase of £513 9a. 7d. 20. per cent. consolidated stock. Th. income from this fund is remitted by the official trustee to the trustee, of the charity in accordance with the terms of the trust.

EARLESTOWN RIOTS.

Trial of Prisoners at Liverpool Assizes.

The hearing was continued at the Liverpool Assizes, yesterday, tefore Mr. Justice Avory, of the case in which four men are indicted for rioting at Earlestown on August 19th. Their names are Peter M`Mion (25) machine erector ; Richard Appleton (30), driller, Frederick Merritt (49), printer ; and David Halms (52) signalmen. According to the:evidence for the prosecution, there was a strike of railway men at Earleetown on August 19, and on that day three signal-boxes were stoned by en excited crowd, two of the signalmen,in fear of their lives, bad to leave their posts. The engines of two trains were also stoned, and one engine-driver had his face cut. klrfalinn was alleged to have gone to one signalman and said : "We have stood this long enough ; you mast come oat of that." Appleton and Merritt were alleged to have joined in the stone throwing, whilst Reline was said to have shouted to the crowd " Lao on, that's it. Let him have it. Fetch him out." . .

Merritt denied be had ever thriwn atones, bat in cross-examination admitted baying had some drink. Appleton oleo denied throwing anything at the sin:al-box. When the constable spoke to him he raid : " You are mistaken, officer. You never saw me throw anything.' said he took no part in the rioting. Canlitre, I the signalman, was a personal friend, and he went into the cabin to warn him of his danger. Hulme said he had been in the company's service for three years. He acted as a picket, but denied inciting anyone to violence. He was not at IVinwick Junction, and did not shout out to the crowd. Szutence was deferred until to day.

Grand Opera In London.

The opening is announced on Monday next of Mr. Oscar Hammerstein's winter seamen of grand opera at the London Opera House, Kingsway—.o event in which many visitors to the metropolis from the provinces will be keenly interested. At the inaugural perform- ance on Monday, "QuolN'adis" will be given, and for Wednesday and Friday "William Tell" and "Norma are announced, while "William Tell" and "Quo Vadia" will be repeated at • matinee and an evening performance on Saturday the 1941. This first week's repertoire will wind up with a grand Sunday evening concert on the 19th inst., further announcements of the works to be performed during the ta. aeon's run of 100 performances being awaited with deep interest by all Interested in operatic matters.

SHAKESPEARE'S HEROINES.

MISS TERRY AT PARR HALL.

Ellen Terry ! What delightful memories are awakened by the writing of me name ! It is over 41 years since the writer first had the felicity of seeing this '' most dear lady" act, and even at that early period of her stage career he was impressed with the wonderful power and charm she displayed— and which milked her out as possessing the characteristic instincts of the really great artist. First impressions are not always to be relied upon to indicate the true bent of the artist's ultimate attain- ments, but there is no doubt in the mind of the writer that in the impersonation of the part he first saw her play, she exhibited all the piquant charm of manner and grace of movement on the stage for which her name has, for a generatiozo, been a symbol. It was in the month of September, in the year 1867 at the Prince's Theatre, Manchester, and the play was Sheridan Knowles's "Hunchback ; " Ellen Terry playing the spirited "Helen" and her sister, Kate Terry (who was then taking her farewell of the stage, prior to her marriage with Mr. Arthur Lewis), the leading character "Julia." So vivid is the writer's recollection of one scene in this play that he can visualise every ur,vement and recall every tone of the voices of these ladies. I Ellen Terrv's statuesque beauty was then " a sight to make an old man young "—her " bounding grace of step," he, "symmetry of mould " gave her an individuality which distingulshed her from all other actresses of her time, and that magnetic influence of .1 her wonderfully sympathetic and appealing voice, together with her sparkling eyes and her unexampled smile, may very truly be said to have captivated all hearts—a style of acting which in its effect upon the auditor was and is unique. On this occasion the "modus" of the piece was the young aspirant, Charles Wyndham, the now famous Sir Charlee— actor and manager. During the many years inter- vening it has been the writer's privilege to see Miss Terry in very many of her famous assutnptions, Ophelia in " Hamlet," Marguerite in " Faust," Olivia, in "Vicar of Wakefield" Beatrice in "Much Ado," and Portia in "Merchant of Venice " being among the most delightful. Miss Terry's lecture was on the " Triumphant Heroines " ef Shakespeare. The revival of old memories by the recital of scenes from " Much ado," "Coriolanns," "As you like it," "Merry 1 Wives," " Merchant of Venice," accompanied by a running comment and analysis of the characters as depicted by Shakespeare was most interesting and afforded real pleasure to the large and appreciative audience assembled in the Parr Hall. Miss Terry exercised all her old fascination and cast the same spell upon her hearers that she has been wont to do any time during her long artistic! career. She simply held them in the hollow of her hand and did what she would with them. To those of us who were able to draw a comparison between her mature powers and the lighter gaiety of her earlier years there was observable a more emphatic and demonstrative expression in many of the familiar passages which gave an impression of formality which in her rendering of the same lines when acting in the plays we had not been conscious of.

The longest and most interesting of her " illustra- , tions " was of " Beatrice " in" Much ado "and her analtsis a this character was most illuminating— whilst the extracts from the scene in which Benedick and Beatrice banter each other—and finally declare their love for one another—were given in the meat exquisitely bewitching manner, quite ler own—for Ellen Terry, like Beatrice was undoubtedly born under a happy star. How her pathetic utterances take hold upon her hearers —the very instant she gets a tear into her voice the lump comes in the throat! All this is only to say that Ellen Terry ml still Ellen Terry:andt hat her personal magnetism is still as great as ever. Over and over again during her reading from her manuscript some little impromptu touch, a remark, a gesture, a look, interrupted our thoughts of the power of the actress to reveal to us the charm of the woman.

Her excerpts from" Coriolanns" and more especially her reading of Faletaff's love-letter from " The Merry Wives of Windsor," showed her more mature() capacity for interpreting the older women in Shakes- peare, whilst her rendering of some of Portia's lines in the trial scene ("Merchant of Venice") reminded the wi iter not so much of Miss Terry herself 30 years ago when he first saw her in this part, as of th 3 great actress who was Miss Terry's earliest instructress—Mrs. Charles Kean, whom the writer had the privilege of seeing as Portia when she was about the same age as Miss Terry is now, and the resemblance between them in the matured period of their lives appeared so him, cn Thursday evening, most striking. The " Mercy " speech was very finely given, though it seemed to be rather a laboured definition of its qualites addressed to the andience, directly, rather than the simple and winning appeal to the heart of the Jew with which Miss Terry acting in the part in years gone by had familiarised us. _ .

Miss Terry had a tremendous welcome when she came on to thecurtained and beflowered platform and a most enthusiastic send-off when she left it. In a few words she thanked the audience for the kindness of her reception on her first visit to Warrington—the name of which for the moment escaped her—the first occasion that the writer has known Miss Terly to require the prompter's help. The recital was given under the auspices of the Warrington Philomathic Society and before Miss Terry appeared,Mr. James Ball, who is the president, gave a short address explaining the origin of the society together with a somewhat humoursoine account of the aims and objects of the society as contrasted I with the distorted notions which he said had gained prevalence in some quarters. He appealed to young men, particularly those who are 'lovers of learning" —though not necessarily 'learned men "—to join the society. The President also made an announcement of a meeting to be Lid at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening next Ur the purpose of forming a Shakespearean Society—the object of which will be the study of the poet's works rather than dramatic representation.

A. PERRIS.

LOCAL WEDDING.

Peacock—Gardner.

There was a pretty weddina at Levratay-pad Wesleyan Church yesterday when Mr. Peacock, jun., son of Mr. Peter Peacock, West-street, Warrington,. was married to Miss May Rita Gardner. daughter of Mrs. L. Gardner, Newton-le-Willows. The Rev W A Beckett officia.ed. The bride was given away by her brother (Mr Wilbert Gardner). She was attired in a handsome blue cloth gown and large black picture hat, ornamented with feathers, and carried a bouquet of white chrysanthemums and orchids. The brides- maid was Miss Brocklehurat who wore a blue velvet I gown with black picture hat. Her bouquet was also composed of white chrysanthemums and orchids. After the ceremony a reception was held at Atkinson's Cafe and later, amid the hearty c•ugratula- tions of their friends, the happy couple left for Liscard. _

Amongst the numerous presents were :—Bride to Bridegroom, gold ring: Bridegroom to Bride, gold bangle ; Mother of Bride, cheque ; Father and Mother of Bridegroom, cheque: Office staff of Messrs. W. D. Houghton, Ltd.. hand-painted Doultoa tea service and pair of massive ornaments.

EMPIRE HALL SKATING RINK.

The popularity of this resort increases every week and considering the enterprising efforts of the management to secure up-to-date attractions, this is not to be wondered at. A balloon race f.,r ladies and a half-mile race for gentlemen aroused great interest. The rink is fully justifying its motto " Always merry and bright.- It is open on Monday. Thursday and Saturday nights and on Saturday afternoon. This (Saturday).evening the final for the half-mile championship will be decided.
